- **Key features:** Search and browse — 'Google-quality capabilities highly tuned for e-commerce and revenue maximization' (verified quote from the Google Cloud blog); Conversational search — 'Enabling real-time, back and forth conversation, powered by Gemini models to guide users through their shopping journeys, while optimizing for conversion'; SEMANTIC IMAGE SEARCH — verified quote: 'Users can search by image (\'find this blouse\') or by a combination of text & image (\'find the shoes that would look great with this blouse\')'. This is a direct hit on CAS's admitted visual-search gap, and the multimodal text+image combination is more advanced than Unbxd's image-upload search.; Recommendations — 'Deliver highly personalized recommendations at scale'; Conversational Commerce — 'helps retailers provide assisted shopping on any digital channel'; Conversational product filtering, tile navigation, faceted search, query expansion, dynamic search (per public docs); Shared data model between recommendations and search — no duplicate ingestion
- **Named clients:** Albertsons Companies — the strongest circumstantial link: Albertsons' AI Shopping Assistant (announced 2025-12-03, live across Albertsons, Safeway, Vons, Jewel-Osco, Shaw's, ACME, Tom Thumb) is reported as a Conversational Commerce agent 'built on Vertex AI' that 'detects shoppers' intent and preferences and chats with them in real-time'. CAUTION: reporting conflicts — PYMNTS (2026-04-13) attributes Albertsons' recipe-to-cart capability to OpenAI. Both may be true for different surfaces. Present as 'Albertsons' AI assistant is reported to use Google Vertex AI', not as a Google case study.; Google's own MQ blog names NO specific customers — it says only that 'our customers have noted our leadership in conversational search'. Do not invent logos here.; Separately reported (search-snippet level, NOT fetched): 'Albertson's Uses Google AI Search to Drive 10% Bigger Grocery Baskets' (cxtoday.com). Treat the 10% figure as UNVERIFIED.
- **Pricing:** NOT VERIFIED. Google Cloud publishes retail/search pricing pages but every fetch attempt this pass returned 404 or truncated content (cloud.google.com/retail/pricing, /vertex-ai-search/pricing, /products/retail/pricing). Structurally it is consumption-based Google Cloud metering (per-query and per-request), typically with Google Cloud free trial credits, but I could NOT extract a number. Do not quote a Google price. This is a gap worth closing before the deck ships.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** Potentially the strongest of any vendor here on BOTH, but under-documented publicly. Grocery: the Albertsons association is directly in the Kroger/Albertsons tier. Fashion: the image-search examples on Google's own page are fashion-native ('find this blouse', 'shoes that would look great with this blouse'). The weakness for a competitive-analysis deck is that Google publishes almost no named retail case studies, so it is hard to evidence — but that is a documentation gap, not a capability gap.
- **Demo/trial:** Google Cloud free-tier/trial credits apply and the APIs are self-serve to any GCP account; full public documentation at https://docs.cloud.google.com/retail/docs/overview. No packaged retail 'free tier' comparable to Algolia's.
- **Vs CAS:** THE MOST DANGEROUS VENDOR THE ORIGINAL REPORT DID NOT NAME, for three reasons. (1) It ships the two things CAS most conspicuously lacks — multimodal image search and Gemini-powered conversational commerce — as commodity cloud APIs. (2) Its integration model is the closest of ANY vendor to CAS's 'sit alongside your existing stack' pitch: it is an API over the retailer's own catalog and event stream, not a storefront replacement, with 'integration time in the order of weeks'. That directly undercuts the original report's central claim that CAS's layer positioning is unique whitespace. (3) Many target retailers are already Google Cloud customers, so it arrives with zero new-vendor friction and pre-approved procurement. WHAT CAS STILL HAS: per-result human-readable reasons and typed hard-constraint enforcement — Google's ranking is a black box by design. HONEST WARNING FOR LATENTVIEW: 'we built semantic retail search' is a hard sell to a CIO who can enable Google's in a sprint. The defensible LatentView story is the consulting wrapper — catalog data quality, eval methodology, and domain tuning — which is precisely what Google's own docs say determines success ('the primary driver for quality search results and performance is the ingested data').

#### Empathy.co (Empathy Platform / Playboard / APISearch / Motive) — https://empathy.co/

- **What it does:** MISSING FROM THE ORIGINAL REPORT, and the single most relevant omission for LatentView's stated target list — because Empathy.co names KROGER and CARREFOUR as customers (verified on empathy.co/customers). European (Spain-based) commerce search and discovery platform whose explicit positioning is privacy-first and infrastructure-independent: 'private and self-hosted cloud under EU governance', data stays on merchant infrastructure, no third-party data sharing, operating independently from major tech providers. Architecturally it unifies traditional keyword search with vector/semantic search in a SINGLE INDEX — the same design as CAS's hybrid router.
- **Key features:** Hybrid retrieval: keyword + vector/semantic search unified in one index; AI Mode for natural-language interaction; AI Overview producing intent-aware summaries with product carousels; AI-generated Related Tags; Next Queries for follow-up suggestions; Playboard — the merchandising console: Results Ranking, query management, facet control, and conversational analytics dashboards on shopper behaviour; Privacy/sovereignty as the product thesis: self-hosted, EU governance, no data sharing with big tech — a genuinely differentiated wedge in European retail; OPEN-SOURCE MODELS on Hugging Face, including GLiNER NER models for BOOKS AND GROCERIES, Qwen3 intent-classification models, and NER datasets — directly reusable reference material for CAS's grocery entity extraction; Three-tier packaging by customer size: Empathy Platform (enterprise), APISearch (medium), Motive (small)
- **Named clients:** Kroger (US GROCERY) — VERIFIED as a named customer on empathy.co/customers. This is LatentView's exact named target account, already served by a vendor the original report never mentioned.; Carrefour (grocery) — VERIFIED as a named customer on empathy.co/customers. Note this contradicts the Fredhopper/Carrefour claim in the Attraqt entry, which is one reason that claim is killed.; Fashion/accessories: Tous (jewellery), PDPAOLA (fashion jewellery), Pronovias (bridal), Perfume's Club; Other: BSH (home appliances), Casa del Libro (books), Conforama (furniture), Toys R Us, Vodafone; IMPORTANT: Empathy.co publishes NO quantitative metrics for these customers on the customers page — logos and testimonials only (e.g. BSH: 'Empathy Platform's merchant-centric design allows our retail brands to truly create a meaningful privacy-based search experience'). Do not attach numbers to these names.
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED. Three named tiers (Empathy Platform / APISearch / Motive) segmented by customer size, but no public prices and no Vendr or marketplace benchmark found. Do not quote a figure.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** EXCELLENT ON BOTH, and uniquely on-target: Kroger and Carrefour for grocery, Tous/PDPAOLA/Pronovias for fashion, all skewed European — matching the H&M/C&A/Zara-tier buyer profile the brief describes. On named-account fit to LatentView's stated targets, this is arguably the best-matched vendor in the entire report, and it was absent from the original.
- **Demo/trial:** No explicit free trial found. Interactive Playboard demos and walkthrough guides are documented on the site. Their open-source models are freely available on Hugging Face — the lowest-friction way to evaluate their approach, and worth downloading for CAS's own grocery NER work regardless of the competitive angle.
- **Vs CAS:** STRATEGICALLY THE MOST IMPORTANT ADDITION TO THIS REPORT. Empathy.co occupies almost exactly the position the original report claimed as CAS's unique whitespace: hybrid keyword+vector in one index, self-hosted on the retailer's own infrastructure, sold as a layer rather than a storefront replacement, with a privacy/sovereignty story instead of a big-tech dependency. That means the original takeaway 'ALL SEVEN ARE RIP-AND-REPLACE, NOT SITS-ALONGSIDE — and that is CAS's only structural whitespace' is FALSE AS WRITTEN. WHAT THEY HAVE THAT CAS DOES NOT: Playboard merchandising console with conversational analytics, AI Overview/Related Tags/Next Queries, multi-tier packaging for different customer sizes, EU data-governance positioning, and published open-source domain models. WHAT CAS STILL HAS: per-product reason strings, typed hard-constraint extraction, ingredient-level recipe decomposition. TWO ACTIONS: (1) study their self-hosted + EU-governance packaging as the closest commercial precedent for how to sell CAS as a layer; (2) if LatentView pitches Kroger, know that Empathy.co is already there.

#### Athos Commerce (consolidation of Searchspring + Klevu) — https://athoscommerce.com/

- **What it does:** MISSING FROM THE ORIGINAL REPORT, and materially changes its consolidation narrative. Klevu — a well-known mid-market AI search vendor — now redirects to Athos Commerce, which also carries Searchspring branding in its login and support areas. In other words TWO established mid-market discovery vendors have been consolidated under one brand, in addition to the Rezolve/Crownpeak deal the original report covered. Athos positions as an AI-powered ecommerce platform for product discovery and sales across multiple shopping channels, notably extending beyond onsite search into feed syndication and AI-channel visibility.
- **Key features:** Site Search with 'intent-based results'; Personalization across search, categories and recommendations; Merchandising for visual curation and product-placement testing; Product Feed Management — syndication to '1,400+ marketplaces, social sites and AI platforms'; Conversational Assistant trained on the retailer's catalog; Channel Assistant for feed optimization across Google and social; GEO Assistant for catalog enrichment — i.e. generative-engine optimization, making the catalog legible to AI shopping agents. NONE of the original seven were described as doing this, and it is a genuinely emerging category CAS does not address at all.
- **Named clients:** Michael Stars (fashion/apparel) — '+951% increase in revenue per visit' and '+15% increase in average order value'. NOTE: the +951% figure is implausibly large for a like-for-like comparison and is almost certainly not a controlled test; cite it only with that caveat, or not at all.; No grocery customers identified on the site.; Klevu's and Searchspring's own historic customer lists were not re-verifiable this pass because klevu.com now serves Athos content. Exact merger/acquisition dates are NOT disclosed on the site — I could not establish when or how the consolidation happened. Flag as an open research item.
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED on the Athos site. Klevu and Searchspring historically both had published or semi-published mid-market pricing, which makes the disappearance of public pricing notable, but I could not verify current figures. Do not quote a number.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** Fashion: yes at the mid-market/DTC tier (Michael Stars is the featured example; both predecessor brands were Shopify/BigCommerce-heavy). Grocery: no evidence. Overall this is a mid-market player, below the H&M/Kroger enterprise tier LatentView targets — relevant as market context and as a consolidation signal more than as a head-to-head competitor.
- **Demo/trial:** No pricing or free-trial information was visible on the site this pass. Both predecessor products historically offered trials via Shopify/BigCommerce app marketplaces — worth re-checking there.
- **Vs CAS:** LOW as a direct competitor, HIGH as market intelligence, for two reasons. (1) It is a THIRD consolidation event alongside Rezolve/Crownpeak, which strengthens rather than weakens the report's 'market is consolidating' thesis — mid-market search vendors are being rolled up, so standalone search-only value propositions are compressing. That is a caution for how LatentView positions CAS: search-only is a shrinking business; the money is moving to the operating layer and to agent-channel visibility. (2) Their GEO Assistant / feed syndication to 'AI platforms' points at a capability none of the other nine vendors here market and CAS does not have: making a retailer's catalog discoverable BY external AI shopping agents, not just by the retailer's own shoppers. If LatentView wants a forward-looking differentiator that is not already commoditized, that adjacency is less crowded than semantic search.

**Segment takeaways:**

- CORRECTION TO THE ORIGINAL REPORT'S HEADLINE CLAIM: 'sits-alongside' is NOT unique whitespace. The original concluded that all seven vendors are rip-and-replace and that CAS's layer positioning is its only structural differentiator. That is wrong on the evidence. Empathy.co (Kroger, Carrefour — both VERIFIED on empathy.co/customers) sells a self-hosted, EU-governed platform that unifies keyword and vector search in a single index and explicitly keeps data on the merchant's own infrastructure. Google's AI Commerce Search is an API over the retailer's own catalog and user events with 'average integration time in the order of weeks' (verified in Google's public docs), not a storefront replacement. Lucidworks Fusion indexes existing enterprise sources via connectors and is self-hostable. So the layer story is real and sellable, but it is CONTESTED, not empty — and one of the contenders is already inside Kroger. Sell the layer on LatentView's integration and eval competence, not on the false claim that nobody else does it.
- BIGGEST COMPLETENESS GAP FIXED: GOOGLE WAS MISSING. The original report cited Google's own Gartner MQ blog in its sources and noted Google was a 2025 Leader, yet never added Google as a vendor. Vertex AI Search for commerce (now 'AI Commerce Search') was named a LEADER in the 2025 Gartner MQ and ships two things CAS admits it lacks: Gemini-powered conversational search, and SEMANTIC IMAGE SEARCH with verified fashion-native examples — 'find this blouse', 'find the shoes that would look great with this blouse'. Because most target retailers already have a Google Cloud contract, it arrives with near-zero procurement friction. Any competitive slide that omits Google will be corrected by the client in the room. I could NOT extract Google's published per-query pricing (all pricing URLs 404'd or truncated) — close that gap before the deck ships.
- CLAIMS I KILLED — DO NOT LET THESE BACK INTO THE DECK. (1) Constructor 'never lost an A/B test to a competitor' — re-checked against BOTH constructor.com/our-proof-schedule and docs.constructor.com; the claim is on neither page. (2) Constructor '40+ enterprise retailers' — not on the AI Shopping Agent page (~27-28 logos, no count claim). (3) Lucidworks 'top five retailer, 91% fewer zero-results, +28% order value' — anonymous, snippet-only, lucidworks.com blocks fetching. (4) Bloomreach's PUMA and Marks & Spencer. (5) Superdry +35% for Fredhopper (implementation-partner marketing page only). (6) Fredhopper's Selfridges/Cdiscount/Mango/Decathlon/M&S/Carrefour list — and Carrefour is specifically contradicted, since Carrefour is a VERIFIED Empathy.co customer. (7) The Algolia '~$50K AWS Marketplace Elevate floor' and Bloomreach '$50k+ annually' secondary-source prices. Two source URLs in the original report are also dead: the Algolia 2025 Gartner press release (404) and coveo.com/en/customers/freedom (404).
- CLAIMS I RE-VERIFIED AND STRENGTHENED. Algolia's entire published price list is exact as reported (Free 10K searches/50K records/5K Recommend/5K crawls; Grow $0.50 per 1K searches + $0.40 per 1K records; Grow Plus $1.75 per 1K searches; NeuralSearch and real-time personalization are ELEVATE-ONLY). Vendr's Algolia data is exact ($41,650 median, $12,413–$260,594, 144 purchases, 23.3% saving) — but caveat it, because other aggregators wildly disagree (one snippet cites a ~$350K average enterprise plan; SpendHound cites ~$11,407 SMB / ~$72,803 enterprise). H-E-B México is exact (34% search speed, 270M+ queries/yr, 88+ stores). Coveo's Freedom Furniture numbers are exact — 5.5% AOV, 15% more sessions with search, 50% decrease in merchandising effort — but the citable source is the PRNewswire release of 2025-05-22, NOT the Coveo merchandising page where they do not appear. Constructor's grocery quotes are exact, including 'plan a week of vegetarian dinners' and the pack-size/volume normalization language.
- CONSTRUCTOR IS A BIGGER THREAT THAN THE ORIGINAL REPORT CONVEYED. Verified via PRNewswire, 2026-06-25: Constructor was named a Leader in the 2026 Gartner MQ 'placing furthest on the Completeness of Vision axis and highest on the Ability to Execute axis among all vendors evaluated' — i.e. the top-positioned vendor in the category, not merely one Leader among several. Coveo and Netcore Unbxd are also verified 2026 Leaders. The same release puts the search and product discovery market at ~$17.41 billion in 2025. Constructor also overlaps CAS on nearly every axis CAS considers differentiating — natural-language cart building, meal-mission intent, dietary substitutions — while additionally shipping KPI-optimized ranking (AOV/LTV/margin), store-level inventory, attribute normalization and clickstream personalization. NOTE: the original report's claim that 'Algolia and Netcore Unbxd repeat as Leaders in the 2026 MQ' is only half-verified — Netcore Unbxd 2026 is confirmed; Algolia's 2026 status I could NOT confirm (search budget exhausted). Do not assert it.
- THE RECIPE DIFFERENTIATOR IS LARGELY GONE — THIS IS THE MOST IMPORTANT STRATEGIC CORRECTION. The original report named ingredient-level recipe decomposition as one of CAS's three defensible claims and called it 'the wedge' in grocery. As of 2026 that is no longer defensible. PYMNTS (2026-04-13) documents Walmart, Tesco and Albertsons all shipping recipe-to-cart: Tesco uses 'two-way dialogue to suggest recipes based on dietary needs' and builds baskets accounting for 'ingredients already at home' (built by Tomoro AI with OpenAI; Tesco also signed a three-year Mistral partnership); Albertsons takes shoppers 'from recipe to cart in under four minutes' versus a typical 46-minute trip and 'imports recipes from uploaded photos'; Walmart's Sparky goes 'from question to cart'. Constructor sells the same surface. CAS's remaining honest differentiators narrow to TWO: (a) a per-product one-sentence human-readable REASON on every ranked result, and (b) TYPED HARD-CONSTRAINT extraction that caps the score ceiling rather than soft-boosting. Lead with explainability and constraint guarantees — not recipes, and definitely not 'semantic search'.
- THE BLOOMREACH/ALBERTSONS REFERENCE IS STALE — RE-BRIEF THE CLIENT LEAD. The original report called Albertsons 'the single most on-target reference in the whole report'. The >25% faster basket-building figure does check out (via search results for the Bloomreach case study; note the case-study URL itself now serves a generic logo wall). But on 2025-12-03 Albertsons announced its own AI Shopping Assistant, now live across Albertsons, Safeway, Vons, Jewel-Osco, Shaw's, ACME and Tom Thumb, reported as built on Google Vertex AI — with PYMNTS separately attributing its recipe-to-cart to OpenAI. Whichever is right, Albertsons' flagship 2026 AI search surface is not being described as Bloomreach. Presenting Albertsons as proof that 'Bloomreach owns grocery AI discovery' invites an easy correction. On current evidence Algolia has the cleaner grocery proof set (H-E-B México, Co-op, Auto Mercado).
- THE GAP IS THE OPERATING LAYER, NOT THE MODEL — AND THIS SURVIVES FACT-CHECKING INTACT. Coveo's verified headline customer outcome is a business-user KPI, not a relevance KPI: '50% decrease in merchandising effort' at Freedom Furniture. Every vendor here ships some combination of merchandising console (Algolia Visual Merchandising Studio, Coveo Merchandising Hub — now with an agentic copilot, Lucidworks Commerce Studio, Unbxd visual workbench, Fredhopper visual merchandising, Empathy.co Playboard), A/B testing, analytics, synonym/rule/query-rewrite editors, autocomplete and personalization. CAS has NONE. Lucidworks' Commerce Studio docs remain the best FREE blueprint (Rules: pin/boost/bury/block/facets/redirects; Rewrites: synonyms, misspellings, phrase rewriting, Head/Tail swap driven by a signals job; Editor with live preview and ranking insights) — and notably even Commerce Studio does not document A/B testing, so the operating layer is not uniformly solved. Build selectively excellent, not complete.
- PRICING ANCHORS — HANDLE THIRD-PARTY BENCHMARKS WITH CARE. Only Algolia publishes real list prices (verified above), and note the commercial trap for them: NeuralSearch is Elevate-only, so any retailer wanting Algolia's semantic search must buy the custom annual top tier. That is CAS's best pricing wedge against the market leader. Coveo publishes metering but not price (100,000 queries/month per unit; 100,000 catalog items baseline; Conversational Search and Generative Answering as separate paid add-ons). Bloomreach: module fee + usage fee, annual only. Constructor, Unbxd, Lucidworks, Crownpeak, Empathy.co and Athos: quote-only. Google: consumption-based but I could not verify a number. Third-party contract data: Algolia median $41,650 (Vendr, 144 purchases — but contradicted by other aggregators), Coveo median $43,621 (Vendr), Constructor ~$150K average with a $200K redline (Vendr, NO sample size disclosed — weaker evidence). A CAS engagement priced in the $40–150K/yr software-equivalent band remains defensible; above $200K it competes head-on with Constructor and loses on features.
- MARKET CONSOLIDATION IS BROADER THAN REPORTED, AND TWO INCUMBENTS REMAIN THE BEST TARGETS. Rezolve AI's Crownpeak acquisition is worse for Crownpeak than the original report showed: announced 2025-12-01 at $90M ($50M cash + $33.9M equity after adjustments) on ~$70M revenue — roughly 1.3x, a distressed multiple — PLUS approximately $150 MILLION IN ASSUMED DEBT, taken on by a buyer whose own annual revenue was just $5.3M. Separately, and missed entirely by the original report, Klevu now redirects to Athos Commerce, which also carries Searchspring branding — a second roll-up consolidating two mid-market discovery vendors. Read together: standalone search-only vendors are being absorbed, and value is migrating to the operating layer and to agent-channel visibility (Athos's GEO Assistant syndicates catalogs to 'AI platforms' — a capability none of the other nine market and CAS does not have). Fredhopper's VERIFIED fashion book — ASOS, JD Sports, Calvin Klein, Tommy Hilfiger, Screwfix, 300+ brands — running on the oldest engine in the field at a vendor mid-ownership-transition, remains the single best-qualified target list in this report.
- RESIDUAL RESEARCH DEBT — WHAT IS STILL UNVERIFIED. Be explicit with the client lead about these rather than papering over them: (1) Google's published per-query pricing (all URLs 404'd/truncated); (2) Algolia's 2026 Gartner MQ status; (3) Bloomreach's 2026 Gartner MQ status; (4) the Athos/Klevu/Searchspring merger dates and structure; (5) Algolia's Agent Studio exact launch date (cited newsroom URL now 404s); (6) most Netcore Unbxd customer metrics, which are vendor-published and were not re-fetched — and which contain a probable transcription error, with '+55.14%' appearing for two unrelated clients. My WebSearch budget was exhausted mid-task (200/200 calls), so items 2-4 could not be closed. Additional vendors worth a future pass that none of this covers: Elastic/Elasticsearch Relevance Engine, Nosto, Luigi's Box, Findologic, Salesforce Commerce Cloud Einstein/Agentforce, and Adobe Commerce Live Search.

**Sources:** https://www.algolia.com/pricing · https://www.algolia.com/products/features/neuralsearch · https://www.algolia.com/products/ai/agent-studio · https://www.algolia.com/customers/h-e-b-mexico · https://www.vendr.com/marketplace/algolia · https://www.spendhound.com/marketplace/algolia-pricing · https://bigsur.ai/blog/algolia-pricing · https://constructor.com/ · https://constructor.com/products/ai-shopping-assistant · https://constructor.com/solutions/grocery-ecommerce-search-and-product-discovery · https://constructor.com/our-proof-schedule · https://docs.constructor.com/docs/make-us-prove-it-the-proof-schedule · https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/constructor-named-a-leader-in-gartner-magic-quadrant-for-search-and-product-discovery--placing-furthest-in-vision-and-highest-in-execution-302810989.html · https://constructor.com/blog/gartner-magic-quadrant-2026-leader

### AI-native / mid-market / SMB product-search vendors and newer LLM-era ("agentic") entrants — FACT-CHECKED AND EXPANDED. Corrections applied: 3 client claims killed as unverifiable (Weis Markets/Instacart, Ocado/Empathy.co, and Empathy.co's specific self-hosted model names), 1 major scope claim narrowed (Empathy.co-Kroger), 2 client names downgraded (UNIQLO, Gymshark), 1 funding stat quarantined (Gensmo $60M). Five vendors added that the original missed: Constructor, Lily AI, Algolia, Syte, and GroupBy/Rezolve. NOTE ON METHOD: this session's WebSearch budget was exhausted before I began, so verification was done entirely by direct page fetch. Where a page blocked automated fetching (403), I say so explicitly rather than passing through the original researcher's claim as verified.

#### Empathy.co — https://empathy.co/

- **What it does:** VERIFIED with one important correction to the original report. Spanish/UK enterprise commerce search vendor built around privacy-first, self-hosted AI. Product tiers: Empathy Platform (enterprise), APISearch (mid-market), Motive (small), plus Visible Privacy. Runs 'AI Mode' conversational search on its own private infrastructure. CORRECTION — the original report claimed Empathy.co serves 'all Search, Navigation and Keyword traffic on Kroger's ecommerce web AND iOS/Android apps.' The actual Empathy.co Kroger page says something narrower: the collaboration began 'nearly five years ago, pre-pandemic,' and 'the Search & Discovery used by all Kroger brands on both desktop and mobile is a product of this partnership,' referencing Kroger's 20+ brands. It does NOT break out Navigation/Keyword traffic or name the iOS/Android apps, and publishes no traffic figures. Use the narrower wording in any deck.
- **Key features:** AI Mode — multi-turn conversational search. VERIFIED from Empathy's own docs: 'Every conversational interaction ultimately resolves into accurate search queries, ensuring that AI-driven discovery remains fully compatible with ranking rules, business logic, and merchandising strategies.' Architecturally this is exactly CAS's translator -> retrieval -> rerank pattern, productised; IMPORTANT DETAIL THE ORIGINAL REPORT MISSED: AI Mode is an OPTIONAL BUTTON that appears when a shopper enters a multi-word query — it supplements rather than replaces keyword search, creating what the docs call 'a graduated transition path from traditional to conversational search.' This is materially close to CAS's keyword-first router, and weakens that as a CAS differentiator; Playboard console — VERIFIED as the merchandiser control surface for the agent; retailers configure 'context and tone to priorities and exclusions'; Runs on proprietary in-house AI infrastructure with self-hosted open-weight models, explicitly independent of third-party LLM APIs, citing data governance and cost control; CORRECTION: the specific model names DeepSeek R1 / Mistral / Qwen3 in the original report are NOT stated on either Empathy page I fetched (the AI Mode doc says only 'Powered by the Empathy.ai technology' plus self-hosted open-weight models). Do not put specific model names in a deck without a primary source; AI Overview — intent-aware summaries with product carousels; AI Related Tags and Next Queries for query refinement; Privacy-first personalization from collective events without storing personal data, plus a Privacy Board for tracking-compliance auditing; OPEN SOURCE: GLiNER NER models including one for GROCERIES, and a Qwen3 Intent model, published on HuggingFace
- **Named clients:** Kroger (VERIFIED — appears in the customer logo section); Carrefour (VERIFIED — case study); Toys R Us (VERIFIED — migrated from Hybris Search); Vodafone (VERIFIED — migrated from Google Search Appliance); Conforama (VERIFIED — explicitly a 'Doofinder to Empathy Platform Migration'); BSH (VERIFIED)
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED anywhere. Three named tiers (Motive -> APISearch -> Empathy Platform) imply a small/mid/enterprise ladder, but no figures are disclosed on any page fetched. Do not estimate.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** STRONGEST COMBINED FIT IN THE SEGMENT AND STILL THE MOST DANGEROUS FOR LATENTVIEW, even after removing Ocado. Grocery: Kroger and Carrefour are both verified and both are literally the target-account profile in the CAS brief. Fashion/lifestyle: Pronovias, PDPAOLA, Tous, Toys R Us. They hold the exact two verticals CAS is being pitched into.
- **Demo/trial:** Interactive demos and Playboard demo guides on-site, video tutorials for Motive, and public technical docs at https://docs.empathy.co/. No self-serve free trial — enterprise sales motion.
- **Vs CAS:** STILL THE SINGLE MOST IMPORTANT VENDOR IN THIS REPORT, and the corrections make the picture slightly better for CAS but not much. They are CAS's architectural twin and they hold Kroger. Two candidate CAS differentiators die here: (1) 'we use open models you can self-host' — Empathy has sold this as Trust by Design for years and open-sources a GROCERY NER model; (2) 'the LLM does not override your merchandising' — their docs state the resolves-to-search-query design explicitly. A THIRD is now also weakened: CAS's keyword-first-router-with-AI-fallback is close to AI Mode's optional-button-on-multi-word-queries design. What CAS still genuinely has: typed hard-constraint extraction that caps the rerank score ceiling, the relevance-gated sponsored-ad layer held out of organic ranking, and recipe -> per-ingredient shopping-list decomposition with brand alternatives. What they have that CAS does not: privacy compliance as a sellable product, the Playboard merchandiser console, AI Overview summaries, and enterprise migration playbooks off Endeca/Hybris/GSA (three verified migrations). If LatentView pitches Kroger, the honest framing is co-existence or displacement of a five-year incumbent, not greenfield.

#### Constructor — https://constructor.com/

- **What it does:** MAJOR GAP IN THE ORIGINAL REPORT — this vendor was entirely absent and belongs near the top of the segment. VERIFIED: AI-native platform focused exclusively on ecommerce product discovery and search, built around a 'Commerce Reasoning Engine' that interprets behaviour, context and intent. Unlike the Shopify-app-shaped vendors, Constructor sells at genuine enterprise retail scale and has shipped a full agent line-up.
- **Key features:** AI Shopping Agent — natural-language product discovery (shopper-facing); Product Insights Agent — answers personalized product questions to build purchase confidence; Merchant Intelligence Agent (Beta) — researches and EXPLAINS SEARCH RANKINGS to the merchandiser. This is a merchant-facing analogue of CAS's per-result reason string and directly targets the 'why did this rank' question; Commerce Reasoning Engine spanning search, browse, recommendations and personalization; Published operating stats: 400+ billion requests powered annually; 8 weeks average implementation time; 98.5% client retention (three-year average); Dedicated fashion/apparel industry solution
- **Named clients:** Sephora; Under Armour; Gap; REI; Tractor Supply; QVC
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED. No pricing disclosed on the site; enterprise sales motion. Do not estimate.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** FASHION VERY STRONG, GROCERY THIN BUT NOT ZERO. Gap, Under Armour, Foot Locker, Sephora, Bath & Body Works, Belk, Rothy's, Bonobos, AG Jeans, White Stuff and Journeys make this arguably the strongest enterprise-apparel reference set in the whole segment — genuinely H&M/C&A-tier, unlike the Shopify DTC vendors. Grocery is represented only by flaschenpost (German delivery) and BJ's (warehouse club), so they are not the grocery threat that Empathy.co and Prefixbox are.
- **Demo/trial:** Demo request only; no public trial or sandbox found.
- **Vs CAS:** HIGH, AND ITS OMISSION WAS THE BIGGEST HOLE IN THE ORIGINAL REPORT. For the fashion pitch this is a top-three competitor alongside Athos and Nosto, and their 8-week implementation plus 98.5% retention are exactly the de-risking numbers a retail CIO weighs against a consultancy build. Their Merchant Intelligence Agent explaining WHY something ranked is the same explainability territory CAS claims. Note also that they publish only business outcomes (RPV, conversion, incremental revenue, ROI multiples) and never IR metrics — reinforcing that CAS's NDCG-led framing will not land. What CAS has that they do not visibly market: relevance-gated sponsored ads separated from organic, recipe-to-shopping-list decomposition, and a published reproducible eval methodology.

#### Lily AI — https://www.lily.ai/

- **What it does:** MAJOR GAP IN THE ORIGINAL REPORT, and the most positionally dangerous omission because their go-to-market is nearly identical to LatentView's. VERIFIED: 'Lily Max' is an AI product-intelligence platform that enriches retailer product catalogues with consumer-language attributes and optimises them across channels, using goal-based agents, then measures impact through controlled A/B testing. They explicitly sell INTO the existing stack: the platform 'optimizes the product intelligence your feed manager, catalog system, and commerce stack already distribute, so there is no replatforming.'
- **Key features:** Catalogue enrichment with shopper-language attributes — attacks the root cause of bad retrieval (thin product data) rather than the ranking algorithm, which is a different and arguably more defensible wedge than CAS's; Goal-based AI agents for catalogue optimisation; Built-in controlled A/B testing to attribute revenue impact — a named CAS gap, and here it is the core proof mechanism, not a side feature; Explicit AI-discovery / agentic-commerce optimisation for ChatGPT, Gemini and AI shopping agents (i.e. a GEO play, the second vendor here with one); Improves both onsite search AND paid channel performance from the same enriched data; Free 30-day trial on 500 products
- **Named clients:** Coach; M&S (Marks & Spencer); J.Crew; Kate Spade; UGG; HOKA
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED. A free 30-day trial on 500 products is offered; no figures disclosed.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** FASHION VERY STRONG, GROCERY NOT EVIDENCED. Coach, J.Crew, Kate Spade, UGG, HOKA, Foot Locker, Vuori, Fabletics and M&S are a serious apparel/footwear roster. M&S is notable as a UK multi-category retailer with a grocery arm, but no grocery capability is claimed on the page I fetched — do not assert grocery for them.
- **Demo/trial:** YES — free 30-day trial limited to 500 products, plus demo request. https://www.lily.ai/
- **Vs CAS:** HIGH STRATEGIC RELEVANCE — they occupy LatentView's exact intended position. CAS's pitch is 'we improve your existing search stack without replacing it'; Lily AI's pitch is literally 'no replatforming,' aimed at the same buyer, with named enterprise fashion logos and A/B-tested revenue attribution already in hand. Their published stats (+28.3% onsite search revenue for a luxury brand, +28% Google Shopping revenue, +21.4% Meta ROAS) are the format CAS's evals must be translated into. The useful lesson: they win the 'integrate, don't replace' argument by proving lift with A/B tests, which CAS currently cannot do. Adding an experimentation capability is more valuable to the CAS roadmap than any additional retrieval tuning.

#### Athos Commerce (Klevu + Searchspring + Intelligent Reach) — https://athoscommerce.com

- **What it does:** VERIFIED. Klevu and Searchspring combined on 13 Jan 2025 to form PSG-backed Athos Commerce, which also absorbed Intelligent Reach. On 10 June 2026 Athos launched a unified 'Intelligent Discovery Platform.' Directly re-verified from athoscommerce.com: the platform modules are Conversational Assistant, Channel Assistant, GEO Assistant and Product Discovery AI (AI & Agents layer); Site Search, Personalization, Merchandising and Product Feed Management (applications); Integration/APIs and Analytics (foundation). Claims 'more than 2,700 global brands' and reach across '1,400+ marketplaces, social sites and AI platforms'; 4.5 stars on G2, 5 stars on Gartner Peer Insights. This merger is the single biggest structural change in the segment — two vendors on the original research list are now one company.
- **Key features:** Conversational Assistant — shopper-facing AI agent, multimodal (text + image upload), follow-up questions, in-dialogue recommendations; GEO Assistant — enriches and syndicates catalogue data for visibility inside ChatGPT and Google Gemini. An entire product category CAS has no equivalent of; Channel Assistant — automated feed optimisation across Google Shopping, Meta, TikTok, marketplaces and retail media networks; AI Search (intent-based), AI Data Enrichment, AI recommendations, AI product bundling, AI-guided merchandising promotions; Legacy Klevu stack: site search, category merchandising, recommendations, merchandiser dashboard, Shopify Markets support; Searchspring-side visual merchandising and campaign builder with drag-and-drop curation
- **Named clients:** adidas; Yamaha; Clarins; Samsung; Rip Curl; Rakuten
- **Pricing:** MIXED, and the published half is VERIFIED EXACTLY. Re-fetched from https://apps.shopify.com/klevu-smart-search : Site Search $649/mo (includes 50k search requests), Recommendations $449/mo (500k impressions, add-on to Site Search), Category Merch $549/mo (250k category page views, add-on). All USD, billed every 30 days, higher tiers available, 14-day free trial on each. Listing shows 4.9/5 from 12 reviews. Athos enterprise pricing is not published anywhere fetchable.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** STRONG FASHION, WEAK GROCERY — unchanged after re-verification. adidas, Rip Curl, River Island, AS Colour and Michael Stars put them in the H&M/C&A/Zara-tier conversation. No grocery retailer named on any page fetched.
- **Demo/trial:** 14-day free trial on all three Klevu Shopify plans (VERIFIED). Enterprise is demo-request only. No public sandbox for the Conversational Assistant.
- **Vs CAS:** TOP-TIER FASHION COMPETITOR. Overlap: intent-based AI search, relevance ranking, recommendations, category merchandising. What they do that CAS does NOT: (1) multimodal/image-upload conversational search — CAS has no visual search; (2) GEO Assistant for ChatGPT/Gemini visibility; (3) feed syndication to 1,400+ channels; (4) a real merchandiser console and AI data enrichment; (5) scale credibility at 2,700+ brands. Their Connected Consumer framing — 60% of consumers use AI tools while shopping, 39% comfortable purchasing through AI platforms (per the 10 Jun 2026 launch coverage) — is the exact framing a LatentView deck will be measured against.

#### Prefixbox — https://www.prefixbox.com/en-us/

- **What it does:** VERIFIED, and its published enterprise pricing re-confirmed exactly. Hungarian enterprise search vendor with an explicit FMCG/grocery vertical. Suite is AI Search + AI Navigation + AI Recommend + an AI Agent (conversational shopping assistant). Claims 60+ clients across nine countries. Unusually for this segment, they publish enterprise list prices.
- **Key features:** AI Shopping Assistant / AI Agent — ChatGPT-like; handles BOTH product discovery and support automation (order tracking, refund status, shipping); Can execute custom ACTIONS including order modifications and refund initiation — true agentic behaviour with side effects; CAS is read-only; 90+ languages with automatic detection; Size & fit guidance derived from product data AND customer reviews; Project-based recommendations for multi-item purchases — structurally the same idea as CAS's recipe shopping list, applied to DIY; FMCG Multistore — auto-updates availability per physical store with per-location shopper analytics. A hard grocery requirement CAS has never addressed; CORRECTION TO ORIGINAL REPORT: the Insights & Experimentation Suite (A/B testing, 2 years of analytics history, OFFLINE SEARCH RELEVANCE MEASUREMENT, and a user-feedback tool) is included at BOTH Grow+ and Enterprise tiers, not gated to the top tier. The only material tier difference is catalogue sync cadence: hourly at Grow+, every 30 minutes at Enterprise; Salesforce AppExchange listing (AI Agent for B2C Commerce)
- **Named clients:** Carrefour; Auchan; Rossmann; Leroy Merlin; Bauhaus (+45% revenue); MediaMarkt
- **Pricing:** PUBLISHED ENTERPRISE PRICES — RE-VERIFIED EXACTLY from https://www.prefixbox.com/en-us/site-search-pricing-for-ecommerce : AI Search EUR 1,250/mo; AI Navigation EUR 550/mo add-on; AI Recommend EUR 550/mo add-on; AI Product Discovery Bundle EUR 2,350/mo, discounted to EUR 2,115/mo. Priced on monthly API requests: Grow+ = 600k-900k requests/mo with hourly full-catalogue indexing; Enterprise = 900k+ with 30-minute indexing. Separately the Shopify app publishes Free ($0 — 500 searches, 1,000 products, 15 AI chats), Essentials $139/mo (34k/4k/400), Standard $229/mo (70k/6k/750), Advanced $389/mo (100k/8k/1,250) at https://apps.shopify.com/prefixbox-search , 5.0/5 from 54 reviews. They meter AI CHATS separately from searches — a pricing model worth stealing.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** BEST GROCERY FIT OF ANY PURE SEARCH VENDOR HERE. Carrefour, Auchan and Rossmann are the Kroger/Albertsons/HEB analogue, and FMCG Multistore shows they have actually solved store-level inventory. Fashion is their weak side — no apparel logo named. If LatentView pitches a grocer, Prefixbox is the incumbent-shaped rival; if a fashion retailer, they are not in the room.
- **Demo/trial:** YES — 30-day free trial per their help centre, 14-day trial on Shopify plans, plus a free Shopify tier. Demo request with 1-business-day response.
- **Vs CAS:** THE MOST DIRECT GROCERY THREAT AND THE BEST PRICING ANCHOR IN THE REPORT — and the correction makes it worse for CAS, because A/B testing and offline relevance measurement are included at EVERY paid tier rather than being a premium upsell. What they do that CAS does NOT: agentic actions with side effects, support-query handling, 90+ languages, size/fit from reviews, store-level availability, A/B testing, and offline relevance measurement tooling. Their published stats are the bar CAS's eval must beat: up to 40% click rate on chat-recommended products; up to $12,000/mo support cost saving; interaction mix of 57% product recommendations / 14% support / 14% product info / 6% promotions. That last breakdown argues a discovery-only assistant — which CAS is — captures barely half the value.

#### Instacart Cart Assistant (Instacart Enterprise AI) — https://company.instacart.com/enterprise-blog/cart-assistant-from-instacart

- **What it does:** VERIFIED, with one client claim killed. Announced 4 Nov 2025 as part of a package of enterprise AI solutions 'to democratize AI for grocers of all sizes.' Cart Assistant is an enterprise-grade, grocery-native AI shopping agent available as a FULLY WHITELABELED solution for retail partners of all sizes, with each retailer's branding shown rather than Instacart's and chat history isolated per storefront. The enterprise page (last updated 12 Aug 2026) states it is now built directly into the Instacart Marketplace for millions of US customers, with full US and Canada rollout planned in the coming months.
- **Key features:** Conversational product discovery with dietary filtering; Cart building and REORDERING through natural-language commands; Recipe and meal planning converted to a cart; Photo AND handwritten-list upload converted into a shoppable list — multimodal input CAS does not have; Real-time inventory AND LOYALTY DATA integration (loyalty was missed in the original report); Personalization learned from past orders and preferences; Three-tiered content moderation system (missed in the original report — relevant because CAS's safety.md flags an unimplemented category blocklist; Instacart has productised moderation); Sibling products announced the same day: Store View (computer-vision shelf inventory), Catalog Engine (AI product-data enrichment), Agentic Analytics, and Agentic Commerce integrations
- **Named clients:** Kroger — Cart Assistant in its iOS app (VERIFIED in the 4 Nov 2025 PRNewswire release); Sprouts Farmers Market — Cart Assistant across website, app and Caper Carts (VERIFIED); Good Food Holdings — Store View, live (VERIFIED); McKeever's — Store View, launching soon (VERIFIED); KILLED: Weis Markets. The original report listed 'Weis Markets (Caper Carts rollout through 2026)' as a Cart Assistant client. The 4 Nov 2025 announcement does not mention Weis at all. Unverifiable in the cited source — remove it; Scale claim VERIFIED: 1,800+ national, regional and local retail banners across nearly 100,000 stores in North America
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED — enterprise partnership motion, almost certainly bundled into the broader Instacart retailer relationship rather than sold as a line item. That bundling is itself the competitive problem.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** GROCERY ONLY, AND IT IS THE DEFAULT ALTERNATIVE A GROCERY CIO WILL RAISE. No fashion relevance whatsoever.
- **Demo/trial:** Live to consumers inside the Instacart Marketplace app today — that is the de facto public demo. No retailer sandbox.
- **Vs CAS:** THE STRUCTURAL THREAT TO THE GROCERY THESIS, and it is not a search vendor at all. A US grocer can get a whitelabeled, grocery-native, inventory-aware AND loyalty-aware agent from a partner it already has a commercial relationship with, bundled rather than separately procured — and Kroger is already piloting it (verified). Everything CAS does in grocery, Cart Assistant does, plus real-time in-stock filtering, photo/handwritten list ingestion, purchase-history personalization, reordering, and content moderation. RECOMMENDATION: do not pitch CAS as a grocery discovery agent head-on. The defensible angle is what Instacart structurally cannot offer — retailer-owned models on the retailer's own infrastructure, no shopper data flowing to a marketplace that is also an intermediary, and integration into the retailer's own keyword stack. That is the same sovereignty argument Empathy.co already wins Kroger with, which is evidence it works.

#### Cooklist — https://www.grocerydive.com/news/cooklist-agentic-ai-grocery-shopping-supermarkets-kroger-wegmans/822950/

- **What it does:** VERIFIED via Grocery Dive, with additional detail the original report missed and one unverified claim flagged. Agentic AI shopping assistant that automates online grocery CART BUILDING — shoppers describe what they need conversationally and receive a cart loaded with suggested products. Announced its agentic shopping technology in June 2026. Integrates into the retailer's existing ecommerce rather than requiring an in-house build, and the retailer retains complete control of customer data rather than ceding it to third-party providers. NOTE: the original report's claim that Cooklist was 'founded 2018 by Daniel Vitiello and Brandon Warman' is not supported by the article I fetched — drop the founder detail unless separately sourced.
- **Key features:** Conversational interface that auto-populates a grocery cart from a described need; Also GUIDES CUSTOMERS THROUGH STORE AISLES and handles SUPPORT QUERIES (missed in the original report — this is broader than a pure basket builder); Roughly SIX-WEEK integration timeline (missed in the original report; a direct competitive benchmark for any LatentView implementation estimate); Recipe app origin, so meal-to-basket is native; Retailer keeps data ownership — the same go-to-market posture as CAS; About half of people using Cooklist-based assistants go INTO A STORE to complete the order — i.e. it bridges online discovery to physical fulfilment; Their stated insight: chat messages average ~50 characters vs ~10 for a search query
- **Named clients:** Wegmans (VERIFIED); Kroger, including the Fred Meyer, Ralphs, Baker's, Dillons, QFC and Smith's banners (VERIFIED); Live across 700+ US stores, with expansion to about 700 more locations 'in the coming weeks' (VERIFIED); Plus 10 additional regional and national banners not yet named (VERIFIED — missed in the original report; the footprint is expanding faster than the report implied)
- **Pricing:** NOT DISCLOSED in the coverage.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** PURE GROCERY, AND IN THE EXACT TARGET ACCOUNTS. Kroger and Wegmans are live. No fashion.
- **Demo/trial:** None public found.
- **Vs CAS:** THE MOST UNCOMFORTABLE FINDING FOR THE CAS GROCERY PITCH, and re-verification made it more uncomfortable, not less. CAS's flagship grocery differentiator — 'ingredients to make pancakes' decomposed into per-ingredient cards with brand alternatives — is functionally what Cooklist already ships, LIVE INSIDE KROGER alongside Empathy.co's search and Instacart's Cart Assistant. Their positioning is nearly identical to LatentView's: integrate into the existing stack, retailer keeps the data. The six-week integration figure is the number any LatentView delivery estimate will be compared against. RECOMMENDATION: stop calling the shopping-list feature novel. Reframe as 'proven demand — Kroger and Wegmans already bought this pattern,' and compete on the per-ingredient brand-alternative carousel being a more merchandisable (and ad-monetisable) surface than a silently auto-filled cart.

#### Luigi's Box — https://www.luigisbox.com/

- **What it does:** CARRIED FORWARD FROM THE ORIGINAL REPORT WITH AN HONESTY CAVEAT: luigisbox.com returns HTTP 403 to automated fetchers, and I could not independently re-verify it in this session. The original researcher fetched it via a browser. CEE-headquartered search & discovery suite with Search, Recommender, Product Listing, Shopping Assistant, Conversational Agent (marked NEW) and Analytics. Claims 4,000+ online businesses and 4.8/5 from 1,000+ reviewers. Positions the Conversational Agent as product discovery, not customer support. Treat all specifics below as single-sourced.
- **Key features:** Conversational Agent — LLM chat built INTO the search bar; appears in search results, in autocomplete, or as a fixed scroll button; multi-turn, desktop + mobile; Clickable refinement 'chips' on every conversational turn — a UX pattern CAS does not have; 'Knows more than your catalog' — markets world-knowledge grounding, mapping an outside-knowledge request to the closest catalogue item AND explaining why. Same context-aware + reason-string claim CAS makes; No-dead-ends: on a zero-result query the agent takes over and redirects; Merchandising in plain language — preferred brands, promoted products and campaign rules set as natural-language instructions; Product carousels rendered inside the conversation; Shopping Assistant (separate, older) — rules-based guided selling with a Dialogue Builder and Multi-Language Sync; their own FAQ concedes it uses AI only 'to a certain extent,' i.e. a quiz engine
- **Named clients:** Kosik.cz — major Czech online GROCERY (single-sourced; case-study page not fetched in either session); Notino; Under Armour; KiK; Skoda; Ziaja
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED — quote-based, priced on usage with a 30-day free trial and no separate charge for core products; base price depends on website usage plus catalogue size; three integration plans (Self-integration / Custom integration / Enterprise) that differ by integration effort and support, not by feature set. Explicitly no free plan. Single-sourced from the original session's browser fetch.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** GOOD ON BOTH ON PAPER, BUT EUROPE-WEIGHTED. Kosik.cz is a genuine online-grocery reference; Under Armour and KiK are apparel. Weak for a US pitch — thin in US grocery (Kroger/Albertsons/HEB) and US fashion.
- **Demo/trial:** Reported 30-day free trial, self-serve, no free plan, plus a live public demo of the Conversational Agent on a real music-store catalogue at https://www.luigisbox.com/products/conversational-agent/. Worth opening in a real browser — it is the closest public analogue to the CAS AI Search page.
- **Vs CAS:** CLOSEST FEATURE-BY-FEATURE ANALOGUE TO CAS'S AI SEARCH PAGE, if the single-sourced description holds. Their Conversational Agent does what CAS does — natural-language intent, catalogue grounding, explanation of why an item matched, zero-result recovery — and adds refinement chips per turn, in-conversation carousels, plain-language merchandising rules (a named CAS gap), multi-language dialogue sync (CAS has no multilingual), and conversation-level analytics. What CAS has that they do not: typed hard-constraint extraction with a score ceiling, a relevance-gated sponsored-ad layer, and a published eval methodology. Their Shopping Assistant being an admitted quiz engine is a usable wedge.

#### Fast Simon — https://www.fastsimon.com/

- **What it does:** VERIFIED, pricing re-confirmed exactly. Shopify-first (but multi-platform) AI search, merchandising, personalization and visual discovery, with a full 'AI Shopping Agent' product line marketing 'natural, engaging conversation a la ChatGPT.' Their own pricing page carries 'Ask Claude / Ask ChatGPT / Ask Perplexity' buttons for prospects — an explicit GEO play.
- **Key features:** AI Shopping Agent (ENTERPRISE TIER ONLY) — Conversational Shopping, Catalog & Shopper Context, Personalization, Guardrails & Configuration, Store Merchandising Compliance, Detailed Analytics. The Guardrails and Merchandising Compliance sub-features productise keeping the agent on-brand and on-policy; AI Visual Discovery (Enterprise) — Visual Search, Shop the Look, Similar/Alternative Products; AI Merchandising Agent and AI Analytics Agent (Enterprise) — merchant-side agents; Ranking Explainer — exposes WHY an item ranked, on every tier. This is the direct analogue of CAS's per-result reason string; Upsell & cross-sell widgets across PDP/home/cart/mini-cart/checkout plus Shopify POS recommendations; Server-side rendering, SDK for web/mobile, 99.99% SLA at Enterprise, B2B customer groups and product entitlement; CORRECTION: the Shopify listing describes Top Pro as adding merchandising rules, A/B testing and VISUAL MERCHANDISING. The original report also credited it with 'multimodal search' at that tier, which the listing I fetched does not state — multimodal/visual discovery is marketed at the higher tiers
- **Named clients:** Steve Madden; Juicy Couture; Hey Dude; Ally Fashion; Francesca's; Mephisto
- **Pricing:** TWO PICTURES, BOTH RE-VERIFIED. Their own pricing page shows Starter/Essential/Growth/Enterprise all as 'Request Pricing' with a free trial. The Shopify listing at https://apps.shopify.com/instant-search publishes: Free (100 sessions/mo), Starter $39.99/mo (2,000 sessions; adds synonyms, custom filters, business analytics), Essential $99.99/mo (10,000 sessions; adds optimized collection filters, SKU search, real-time sync), Top Pro $299.99/mo (30,000 sessions; adds merchandising rules, A/B testing, visual merchandising). Annual billing saves 17%. 4.8/5 from 289 reviews.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** FASHION YES, GROCERY NO. Client list is almost entirely apparel/footwear/accessories. Zero grocery signal. DTC-scale rather than H&M-scale.
- **Demo/trial:** YES — free tier on Shopify (100 sessions/mo) plus a free trial on paid tiers, and demo scheduling.
- **Vs CAS:** BEST EXAMPLE OF HOW THE MARKET PACKAGES AN AI SHOPPING AGENT, and their feature matrix doubles as a gap checklist: CAS has none of A/B testing, visual/image search, personalization, SSR, merchant-side agents, B2B customer groups, or an SLA. They gate the AI Shopping Agent behind Enterprise — evidence conversational agents are sold as premium upsell, not commodity. Most important single fact: their Ranking Explainer ships on EVERY tier at $39.99/mo, so CAS's per-result reason string is already commoditised and cannot carry a differentiation slide. What CAS has that they visibly do not: a relevance-gated sponsored-ad layer auditably separated from organic, and recipe-to-shopping-list decomposition.

#### Doofinder — https://www.doofinder.com/en/

- **What it does:** VERIFIED, pricing re-confirmed exactly. Spanish SMB-focused site search that has moved up-market into conversational. Claims 10,000+ stores and 30+ platform integrations. Products: AI-powered search (intent, typos, images), AI Assistant (conversational commerce), recommendations, category merchandising, visual search, searchandising, AI insights, voice search, autocomplete.
- **Key features:** AI Assistant — conversational shopping marketed with an example almost identical to CAS's pitch ('Going to the beach with kids—any toy ideas?') parsed for goals, context, colours and price ranges; In-chat side-by-side product COMPARISON — CAS has no comparison view; Also answers support/FAQ questions (returns, shipping, payment) — CAS is discovery-only; Multi-language built in; Visual / image search on the Advanced tier; AI Insights — surfaces trends and opportunities to the ecommerce manager; Quiz Maker included in every paid tier
- **Named clients:** Crocs; Volkswagen; Blue Banana; Eurekakids (AI Assistant handles 40% of pre-purchase questions); Prenatal; Iturri
- **Pricing:** PUBLISHED AND CHEAP — RE-VERIFIED EXACTLY from https://www.doofinder.com/en/price : Basic $49/mo (up to 10k requests/mo per product), Pro $149/mo (150k), Advanced $349/mo (400k), Enterprise custom. 10% annual discount ($44/$134/$314). All plans include Search, Recommendations and Quiz Maker. The AI Assistant is available on Pro/Advanced/Enterprise but priced SEPARATELY — the page says 'Get in touch for details and pricing,' so the headline $149 does NOT include the conversational agent. Several third-party sites still quote a stale $39/$59/$129 ladder — use the vendor page.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** SMB-to-lower-mid-market, Southern Europe/LatAm weighted. Crocs is the only real fashion name. Farmasave/Freshly are the closest to grocery/FMCG and neither is a supermarket. NOT a credible bidder for Kroger or H&M — but they set the low price anchor the whole market gets compared against.
- **Demo/trial:** YES — free trial, no credit card required, plus demo booking.
- **Vs CAS:** Relevance is mostly COMMERCIAL. Their conversational assistant covers similar ground at a $149-349/mo base, which is the number a LatentView build must justify itself against below enterprise — though note the AI Assistant is a separate line item, so the true comparable is higher than the headline. What they do that CAS does not: in-chat product comparison, support/FAQ answering, visual search, a quiz builder and a merchandising console. Useful competitive-intel detail, now independently confirmed: Empathy.co's customers page describes Conforama as a 'Doofinder to Empathy Platform Migration' — direct evidence the cheap tier does not hold at large-catalogue enterprise scale, which is exactly the argument CAS should make.

#### Nosto — https://www.nosto.com/

- **What it does:** VERIFIED with two client names downgraded. Commerce Experience Platform built on 'experience.AI' — personalization first, search second. Two module clouds (Product Experience Cloud and Content Experience Cloud) on a shared data engine, plus 'Huginn,' an AI agent for the MERCHANT rather than the shopper.
- **Key features:** Huginn — 'your AI agent for commerce experiences' that 'coordinates a network of purpose-built AI agents that work autonomously to expand your team's capacity, boost efficiency, and elevate customer experiences' (VERIFIED wording). Launched 9 Oct 2025; Agentic AI workflows for Shopify Sidekick (28 May 2026) — merchandisers adjust discovery and personalization conversationally inside Shopify; Product Experience Cloud: Personalized Search, Category Merchandising, Product Recommendations, Post-Purchase Upsell, Dynamic Bundles, Personalized Emails; Content Experience Cloud: A/B Testing & Optimization, Content Personalization, Behavioral Pop-Ups, Shoppable UGC; Four named AI capabilities: Predictive, Semantic, Visual and Generative AI
- **Named clients:** VERIFIED on the CXP page: Muji, Belstaff, Diane von Furstenberg, Figs, Vuori, Kylie Cosmetics, SKKN by Kim, Douglas, Dermalogica, Diptyque, O'Neill, Oh Polly, Tog24, Arhaus, Tuckernuck, plus Bylt, Butlins, Philosophy, Jaded and Daals (the last five were missed in the original report); DOWNGRADED: UNIQLO and Gymshark. Both were in the original report, sourced to a Nosto-vs-Algolia blog post rather than a customer page. Neither appears in the logo set on the Commerce Experience Platform page I fetched. Do not lead a deck with UNIQLO — it is the single most impressive name on that list and the least verified
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED as figures, but the MODEL is notable: Nosto's own comparison content states pricing is FIXED BY GMV so merchants use search and discovery WITHOUT QUERY-BASED LIMITS. That claim comes from Nosto's own competitive blog page (not re-fetched this session) — treat the model as directional, the absence of published figures as certain. The Shopify app is free to install but requires a paid Nosto account (4.8/5, 51 reviews).
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** FASHION/BEAUTY STRONG, GROCERY ABSENT. Even after removing UNIQLO and Gymshark, Muji, Belstaff, DVF, Figs, Vuori and Oh Polly are a serious apparel/beauty set. No grocery retailer appears anywhere.
- **Demo/trial:** Demo request only; no public free trial. Shopify app is free to install but non-functional without a paid account.
- **Vs CAS:** Important because they define the OTHER kind of AI agent. Huginn is merchant-side — it works for the merchandising team, not the shopper. CAS has neither a shopper-facing conversational agent surface NOR any merchant-side agent, and the 'no merchandising console, no analytics dashboard' gaps map exactly onto what Huginn sells. Their GMV-based, query-unlimited pricing model is a direct counter to the per-request metering CAS inherits from an LLM cost structure — worth pre-empting, because CAS's ~7s warm latency and per-query LLM cost make uncapped pricing structurally hard. What CAS has that Nosto does not lead with: deep query understanding and constraint handling; Nosto's centre of gravity is behavioural personalization, precisely where CAS admits it has nothing.

#### Marqo — https://www.marqo.ai/

- **What it does:** VERIFIED — all client statistics re-confirmed on https://www.marqo.ai/ecommerce. Melbourne/London/SF vector-search company founded by ex-Amazon engineers, repositioned from infrastructure into an 'AI-native ecommerce search and product discovery platform.' Distinctive claim: 'Marqo trains a dedicated AI model on your real product catalog and shopper behavior' — 'Your model,' not a shared LLM. Broader positioning is 'Commerce Superintelligence.'
- **Key features:** MarqTune — fine-tunes embedding models on the retailer's own catalogue using Generalized Contrastive Learning, trained on REAL purchase signals rather than synthetic data; Marqo-Ecommerce-B and Marqo-Ecommerce-L — open multimodal product embedding models on HuggingFace with published benchmarks; 'LLM based image search integrated directly into the search model,' including reverse image search; Discovery surfaces named as Search, Browse, Recommendations and 'Agentic Storefront'; Open-source core (Apache 2.0, self-hostable) plus Marqo Cloud managed tier; One-click integrations for Shopify, Adobe Commerce and Salesforce Commerce Cloud
- **Named clients:** Mejuri (+19.8% search revenue per user) — VERIFIED; Redbubble (+$11M incremental revenue) — VERIFIED; KICKS CREW (+17.7% conversion uplift) — VERIFIED; Shutterstock (+23% search satisfaction) — VERIFIED; SwimOutlet (+10.6% search add-to-cart rate) — VERIFIED; Kogan (+15.5% search revenue per visitor) — VERIFIED
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED — marqo.ai/pricing redirects to a book-a-demo page. Open-source self-hosted is free (Apache 2.0); Marqo Cloud is usage-based with a free trial.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** FASHION/LIFESTYLE YES, GROCERY NO. Mejuri (jewellery), KICKS CREW (sneakers), SwimOutlet (swimwear), Redbubble and Temple & Webster are fashion/home. They target fashion, beauty, electronics and home goods. Zero grocery — visual/multimodal search matters far less for a weekly grocery basket, which is likely why.
- **Demo/trial:** BEST TECHNICAL ACCESS OF ANY VENDOR HERE: open-source Marqo on GitHub, ecommerce embedding models downloadable at https://huggingface.co/Marqo, a public benchmark repo, and a Marqo Cloud free trial. Commercial platform is demo-request only.
- **Vs CAS:** THE MOST DIRECT TECHNICAL THREAT TO CAS'S RETRIEVAL LAYER. CAS's documented decision was to benchmark off-the-shelf small encoders and pick gte-small. Marqo's entire pitch is that off-the-shelf embeddings are the ceiling and a catalogue-fine-tuned multimodal model beats them — and they publish open weights and benchmarks to argue it. The CAS working tree already contains scripts/finetune_embeddings.py, so this is a live decision, not hypothetical. What they do that CAS does not: catalogue-specific fine-tuning on behavioural signals, multimodal/reverse image search, and open published retrieval benchmarks. What CAS has: LLM reranking with human-readable reasons, typed hard-constraint enforcement, recipe decomposition and the sponsored layer. Their client stats are also the exact format CAS's evals should be translated into — revenue per user and add-to-cart rate, not NDCG.

#### Algolia — https://www.algolia.com/pricing

- **What it does:** GAP IN THE ORIGINAL REPORT — omitted despite being the most-referenced price anchor in the category and now shipping agentic features. VERIFIED from their pricing page: developer-first search-and-discovery API with published, self-serve, usage-based pricing and a genuine free tier. Named AI features include NeuralSearch, AI Synonyms, AI Ranking, Advanced Personalization, Dynamic Re-ranking, AI Collections, Smart Groups, and 'Agent Studio.'
- **Key features:** Agent Studio — their agentic layer, marketed as 'AI powered by Algolia'; NeuralSearch — hybrid keyword + vector retrieval in one engine (Elevate tier). Architecturally the same hybrid bet as CAS's BM25 router plus embeddings; AI Synonyms and AI Ranking (Grow Plus tier) — automated relevance tuning; Advanced Personalization (Grow Plus) and Real-time personalization (Elevate); Query Categorization, Collections, Dynamic Re-ranking; 99.99% availability, enhanced SLA and enterprise support at Elevate; Analytics retention laddered by tier: 30 days (Grow), 90 days (Grow Plus)
- **Named clients:** Not extracted from the pricing page I fetched. Algolia publishes an extensive customer list elsewhere, but I did not fetch it this session — do NOT assert Algolia logos in a deck without a primary-source check
- **Pricing:** PUBLISHED AND FULLY USAGE-BASED — VERIFIED from https://www.algolia.com/pricing : Free (10K search requests/mo, 50K records, 5K recommendation requests, 5K crawls, no credit card). Grow (10K requests included then $0.50 per additional 1K; 100K records then $0.40 per 1K; keyword search, query suggestions, manual synonyms, 30-day analytics). Grow Plus, pay-as-you-go (10K requests included then $1.75 per additional 1K; same record pricing; adds AI Synonyms, AI Ranking, Advanced Personalization, Query Categorization, Collections, 90-day analytics). Elevate (annual contract, custom volume pricing with discounts; adds NeuralSearch, AI Collections, Smart Groups, real-time personalization, 99.99% availability). Note the AI tier costs 3.5x per incremental 1K requests ($1.75 vs $0.50) — the market prices AI relevance at roughly a 3.5x premium over keyword, a useful anchor for CAS pricing conversations.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** HORIZONTAL, NOT VERTICALISED. Algolia sells to any ecommerce or content site; there is no fashion- or grocery-specific product on the pricing page. They are less likely to win a grocery RFP on vertical fit than Prefixbox or Empathy.co, but they are frequently the incumbent a retailer already has, which makes them the displacement target rather than the bid competitor.
- **Demo/trial:** YES — genuine free tier with no credit card required, plus self-serve paid tiers. The most frictionless technical evaluation in the segment.
- **Vs CAS:** Belongs in the report for three reasons the original missed. (1) PRICE ANCHOR: their $0.50-vs-$1.75 per 1K request ladder is the cleanest public evidence of what the market will pay for AI relevance over keyword relevance — roughly 3.5x — which is directly usable in a CAS pricing discussion. (2) ARCHITECTURE: NeuralSearch is hybrid keyword+vector in one product, so CAS's 'keyword-first router with AI fallback' is a variation on a shipped commercial pattern, not a novel design. (3) INCUMBENCY: for a mid-market fashion or grocery retailer, Algolia is very often what is already installed, so the realistic CAS engagement is 'layer onto or displace Algolia,' making their feature set the practical baseline. Also note Nosto markets specifically against Algolia on query-limit pricing — that comparison is a live sales battleground CAS will be dropped into.

#### Syte — https://www.syte.ai/

- **What it does:** GAP IN THE ORIGINAL REPORT — omitted even though the report itself names visual search as one of four universal CAS gaps. VERIFIED: AI-powered product discovery platform for apparel ecommerce, describing itself as 'the only AI-powered product recommendation and discovery platform built from the ground up for ecommerce.' Visual-AI-first rather than LLM-first.
- **Key features:** Camera/image search — shopper photographs or uploads an image to find matching catalogue products; Inspiration galleries and 'Shop the Look' / 'Shop the Room'; 'Shop Similar' and 'Shop Social' visual recommendation engines; AI product tagging and automated attribute extraction feeding merchandising; Hyper-personalized recommendations driven by visual attributes; NOTABLE ABSENCE: no conversational or agentic AI is marketed on their homepage — they are a visual-discovery specialist, not an LLM-agent vendor. This is the mirror image of CAS, which has language understanding and zero vision
- **Named clients:** Prada; Farfetch; PrettyLittleThing; Decathlon; Castorama; Chow Sang Sang
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED. No pricing on the site; enterprise/mid-market sales motion.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** FASHION AND HOME ONLY, NO GROCERY. Prada, Farfetch, PrettyLittleThing and Decathlon are a strong apparel reference set spanning luxury, fast fashion and sport. Visual search has essentially no grocery use case, which is consistent with every visual vendor in this segment avoiding the vertical.
- **Demo/trial:** Demo request only; no public trial or sandbox found on the homepage.
- **Vs CAS:** Relevant as the SPECIALIST PROOF that visual search is a standalone, enterprise-fundable category in fashion — not merely a checkbox inside a broader suite. When the original report flags 'no visual/image search' as a CAS gap, Syte is what that gap looks like when a serious buyer goes shopping for it, with Prada and Farfetch as evidence that luxury and marketplace fashion both pay for it. Two implications for LatentView: (1) in a fashion pitch, image input is now table stakes rather than advanced, and CAS has no answer; (2) conversely, Syte having no conversational/agentic layer shows the market is still split between vision-first and language-first vendors — a combined language + vision pipeline is a genuine, defensible CAS roadmap item rather than a me-too feature.

#### GroupBy (now absorbed into Rezolve AI) — https://www.groupbyinc.com/

- **What it does:** NEW CONSOLIDATION EVENT THE ORIGINAL REPORT MISSED. DIRECTLY OBSERVED THIS SESSION: https://www.groupbyinc.com/ returns HTTP 301 Moved Permanently to https://www.rezolve.com/ — GroupBy no longer operates its own web presence. GroupBy was a Toronto-based ecommerce search and merchandising vendor best known as the leading implementation partner for Google Cloud's Vertex AI Search for Commerce, with a strong grocery/B2B distribution client base. CAVEAT ON CONFIRMATION: rezolve.com and its news pages return HTTP 403 to automated fetchers and investors.rezolve.com does not resolve, so I could NOT retrieve a press release confirming the acquisition date or terms. The domain redirect is verified fact; 'acquired by Rezolve AI' is my inference from that redirect and should be confirmed with a primary source before it appears in any client-facing material.
- **Key features:** Historically: ecommerce site search, merchandising console and product discovery built on Google Cloud Vertex AI Search for Commerce; Merchandiser-facing rule and curation tooling; Strong presence in grocery and B2B distribution catalogues; Rezolve AI positions itself as an agentic/conversational commerce AI company, so the combined entity is another instance of a classic search vendor being absorbed into an LLM-era platform
- **Named clients:** NOT VERIFIED THIS SESSION — the original GroupBy customer pages are gone with the domain and rezolve.com blocks automated fetching. I am deliberately naming no clients rather than passing through recalled ones. Any GroupBy logo used in a deck must be re-sourced from a live Rezolve page or press release
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED and not verifiable following the redirect.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** HISTORICALLY GROCERY-LEANING AND B2B DISTRIBUTION, which is why the omission matters for the grocery pitch — but with the site gone I cannot evidence current client fit. Treat as a market-structure datapoint, not as a live competitor profile.
- **Demo/trial:** N/A — the standalone product and site no longer exist independently.
- **Vs CAS:** Relevance is STRATEGIC and it strengthens the report's own headline. The original report counted three of thirteen vendors as no longer independent (Klevu, Searchspring, Vantage, Findify). GroupBy makes it worse: a fourth established search vendor has disappeared into an agentic-AI acquirer, and it was one of the grocery-relevant ones. For LatentView this cuts two ways — it confirms that standalone search vendors are not durable businesses (supporting 'CAS is a consulting capability, not a product'), and it removes a potential grocery competitor while showing that Google's Vertex AI Search for Commerce remains the build-vs-buy substrate a retailer's own team may reach for instead of any vendor. ACTION: someone should confirm the Rezolve transaction from a primary source before this appears in a deck.

#### Searchspring (a division of Athos Commerce) — https://searchspring.com

- **What it does:** VERIFIED as no longer independent. Founded 2007, PSG portfolio company doing ecommerce site search, merchandising and personalization, heavily indexed on Shopify Plus. Merged with Klevu in Jan 2025; the domain still serves case studies but the brand is presented as 'a Division of Athos Commerce.' Treat as the same buyer conversation as Athos, not a separate one.
- **Key features:** Site search with autocomplete and faceting; Visual merchandising / campaign builder with drag-and-drop curation and merchandising rules; Personalization (Searchspring-branded release, Mar 2025); Analytics and search-term reporting; Deep Shopify Plus / BigCommerce / Magento integration
- **Named clients:** Hat Club; SuperATV; MacSales; Costumebox; AS Colour; Thule
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED by the vendor. The original report cited third-party trackers quoting roughly $599-1,099/mo. I could not verify those against any Searchspring or Athos page. RECOMMENDATION: cut this range entirely from any client material — an unverifiable competitor price is worse than saying 'not published.'
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** Mid-market fashion/apparel and DTC, US-centric. No grocery presence found. Below the H&M/C&A tier on its own; the enterprise story is now the Athos story.
- **Demo/trial:** Demo request only. No public trial for the Searchspring-branded product.
- **Vs CAS:** Overlaps CAS on search plus merchandising, but the durable point is strategic rather than technical: Searchspring's absorption is evidence that standalone mid-market search vendors are being rolled up. Its real differentiator versus CAS is the merchandiser-facing campaign console and visual merchandising UI — a named CAS gap. Searchspring's entire commercial value proposition was that a non-technical merchandiser could operate it without engineering, which is precisely the capability CAS lacks and the one a retail buyer will probe first.

#### Findify (now Maropost Merchandising Cloud) — https://maropost.com/platform/merchandising-cloud

- **What it does:** VERIFIED, pricing re-confirmed exactly. findify.io redirects to maropost.com/platform/merchandising-cloud and that page still references status.findify.io — Findify is fully absorbed into Maropost. Maropost acquired the Stockholm-founded (2014) startup for just over US$4.45M, announced 9 June 2022. The product is AI-ranked search, smart collections, recommendations and merchandising rules. IMPORTANT DISAMBIGUATION worth keeping: this is NOT the Shopify acquisition — the March 2025 'Shopify acquires AI search startup' story is Vantage Discovery, a different company.
- **Key features:** AI-ranked search with typo tolerance and synonym matching; Smart Collections — auto-rank category pages by popularity, margin, inventory; Behaviour-based product recommendations; Merchandising rules: boost, bury, pin, exclude; Real-time sync with Shopify, BigCommerce, Neto, Norce; Claims +26% search conversion, +7% AOV, +17% conversion on Smart Collections
- **Named clients:** Historic (from acquisition-era coverage): Victoria Beckham, Gina Tricot, Everlast, James Perse, Stronger, Aim'n; Current Maropost page names much smaller accounts: Wall Tools, Icon by Design, Storiarts, UroTuning, Lisen.dk, Cyberphoto, TradeWins, Altapac
- **Pricing:** PUBLISHED, RE-VERIFIED EXACTLY from https://apps.shopify.com/findify-search : Premium $499/mo ($5,400/yr with 10% discount — AI personalized search and autocomplete, up to 100k visits/mo, up to 20k products, single store/language), Professional $799/mo ($8,650/yr — adds Smart Collections, up to 50k products, multi-store/language), Enterprise $1,399/mo ($15,100/yr — adds personalized recommendations, custom visit limits, unlimited products). 14-day free trial on all tiers. 4.6/5 from 65 reviews.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** HISTORICALLY FASHION but the momentum is gone — currently-named accounts are small industrial and DTC shops. No grocery. Effectively out of the enterprise fashion race.
- **Demo/trial:** YES — 14-day free trial on all Shopify plans.
- **Vs CAS:** LOW as a live competitor, HIGH as evidence, and the evidence is sharper than the original report framed it. The Maropost page has NO conversational assistant at all — it is a pre-LLM ranking and merchandising product, showing how fast an ML-era search vendor becomes dated.
