- **Named clients:** Alpha Industries — +5.7% conversion rate, +6.3% total orders, achieved in under three months, with an attributed quote from Matthew Pantoja, VP Global Marketing and E-Commerce [V]; "250+ global partners" claimed [V]; Calvin Klein, Vans, Intersport, The Iconic, John Lewis, Ganni — from TechCrunch coverage of the 2021 Snap acquisition [S], NOT confirmed on the current site; these are five-year-old client references and should be treated as historical.
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED. Corporate history for context: Snap acquired Fit Analytics in March 2021 for a reported $124.4M, and the company has since decoupled from Snap and operates independently [S — TechCrunch and a Fit Analytics press release PDF that could not be text-extracted]. *** NOTE *** The current homepage makes NO mention of Snap or of independence [V], so do not present the decoupling as a company-promoted fact. Pre-acquisition press figures (18,000 retailers, 100M+ users, 4B+ recommendations/month) are historical — do not quote them as current.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** FASHION: essential and category-defining — apparel and footwear only. GROCERY: none.
- **Demo/trial:** No public trial; demo/contact-sales. The widget is observable in the wild on client PDPs — a cheap way to see it working without a sales call.
- **Vs CAS:** COMPLEMENT AND PARTNER CANDIDATE, WITH ONE ENCROACHMENT — original judgement verified and the supporting stats all confirmed on the primary page. *** THE RETURNS STATISTIC IS NOW PROPERLY SOURCED, AND THE ORIGINAL'S VERSION WAS TOO VAGUE. *** The original said "one industry source puts fit issues at over 50% of apparel returns." Better framing, with real attribution: a 2023 Coresight Research survey of 100 US apparel decision-makers found 53% of apparel returns are driven by sizing; a 2024 McKinsey study found up to 30% of online fashion purchases are returned with incorrect sizing accounting for ~70% of those. [S — both via secondary summaries; pull the primary Coresight/McKinsey reports before quoting either]. Estimates range from ~30% to ~70% depending on methodology, so present a RANGE and name the source, never a single bare figure. STRATEGIC CALL UNCHANGED: partner, do not build. The moat is a decade of body/garment/return data, not algorithms. Position CAS as fit-vendor-agnostic and explicitly compatible. THE ENCROACHMENT IS REAL AND CONFIRMED: their AI Shopping Assistant handling style, budget and context is CAS's exact territory, arriving from the fit side. Fit vendors answer "which size of THIS item"; CAS answers "which item" — but that boundary is eroding and CAS should stake the discovery claim before it closes.

#### Bold Metrics — https://boldmetrics.com/

- **What it does:** AI body-data platform for apparel sizing requiring NO body scan, photo or measuring tape — infers a "digital twin" mapping 50+ individual body measurements from minimal inputs, then matches against per-style garment specs.
- **Key features:** Smart Size Chart replacing the static size chart with an individualized recommendation [V]; Virtual Sizer, Virtual Tailor, Apparel Insights [V]; Agentic Sizing Protocol — positioned as "The Missing Link in Agentic Commerce"; sizing exposed for AI agents [V]; Style-by-style sizing rather than brand-level — the actual failure mode in apparel [V]; Scale claims confirmed on the homepage: 250+ million digital twins, 12+ billion body data points, 50+ measurements per twin, 25+ brands [V]
- **Named clients:** CONFIRMED on the homepage: New Balance, Charles Tyrwhitt, Canada Goose, Pact, Vuori, Amsale, Mizzen + Main, Suit Shop, Simms, French Toast, Helly Hansen, Meshki, Berne Apparel, BirdyGrey, Lakeland Fire, Filson, American Tall, Beyond Yoga, Birddogs, Sun Day Red, Tommy Hilfiger School Uniforms, Born Primitive [V — this is a broader and better-verified list than the original report's]; Men's Wearhouse 47.4% average return-rate decrease on online tuxedo rentals — from a Bold Metrics blog via search result [S], not confirmed on the homepage; attribute carefully or drop.; NOTE the scale nuance: "25+ brands" is a modest customer count against Fit Analytics' "250+ global partners." Bold Metrics is the challenger here, not the incumbent.
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED; demo-request CTAs only, no tiers or figures anywhere on the site. [V] They publish a free "Fit & Sizing Technology Buyer's Guide" at https://info.boldmetrics.com/fit-sizing-technology-buyers-guide — a useful artifact for understanding how fashion buyers evaluate this category.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** FASHION: apparel-only, strong in menswear, outdoor, performance, workwear and uniforms. GROCERY: none.
- **Demo/trial:** Demo request only, no public trial. [V]
- **Vs CAS:** COMPLEMENT AND PARTNER CANDIDATE, not a competitor — judgement verified, all headline stats confirmed on the primary page. The strategically interesting detail remains the no-scan approach: it removes the friction that has capped adoption of camera-based sizing, which means fit signal could become ubiquitous enough to be a RANKING FEATURE rather than just a PDP widget. If a retailer knows a shopper's inferred measurements, "show me a blazer for a summer wedding" should filter to items that actually fit them IN STOCK — CAS could consume a fit signal as a hard constraint in its existing typed-constraint framework. That is a genuinely differentiated integration story and cheap to build given the constraint plumbing already exists. THEIR "AGENTIC SIZING PROTOCOL" IS NOW PART OF A CLEAR SEGMENT-WIDE PATTERN that the original report noticed but under-weighted: Bold Metrics (Agentic Sizing Protocol), YesPlz ("GEO Ready"), Athos Commerce (GEO Assistant, feeds to "AI platforms"), Lily AI (schema markup for AI answer engines), Algolia ("agentic discovery"). Making the catalog legible to AI shopping agents has become a standard product line across this entire market in roughly twelve months. See the takeaways — this is now a named CAS gap.

#### Vue.ai (Mad Street Den) — https://www.vue.ai/

- **What it does:** Formerly a fashion-first retail AI suite (VueTag automated catalog tagging, personalization, on-model imagery). HAS PIVOTED AWAY from retail specialization to a horizontal enterprise AI platform — verified again in this pass and, if anything, more pronounced than the original report described.
- **Key features:** Now positions as "The Only AI Platform You Will Ever Need" across four hubs: Data Hub (data cleanup, product tagging, content moderation), Customer Hub (personalization, audience building, journey orchestration), Automation Hub (intelligent document processing, workflow automation), Optimization Hub (sales efficiency, lead generation) [V]; *** THE PIVOT IS NOW EXPLICIT IN THEIR VERTICAL LIST: eCommerce/retail, FINANCIAL SERVICES, INSURANCE, LOGISTICS and HEALTHCARE *** [V] — fashion is now one line item among five industries; VueTag fashion tagging heritage: 35+ apparel and accessory product types, attributes including colour, pattern, length, neckline, sleeve-type [S]; Efficiency argument reusable in CAS's own pitch: manual labeling consumes ~30 hours per person per week to tag only 200-300 products; AI digitization gets products to market 10X faster [S]; "30:60:90 promise": Day 30 pilot go-live, Day 60 ROI proof, Day 90 scale [S]
- **Named clients:** CONFIRMED on the current homepage: HDFC Bank, Tata Motors, Microsoft, Google Cloud, Zenyum, thredUP, Dubai CommerCity, TDL [V]; Note how few are fashion retailers — thredUP is the only remaining fashion-adjacent logo prominently displayed. Diesel, cited in the original report, did NOT appear in this pass. Drop it unless re-verified.; thredUP founder Chris Homer testimonial [S — historical]
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED. Claims "At a fraction of the cost. At 5X the speed" without figures. [S]
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** FASHION: historically strong but now clearly de-emphasized — the homepage sells to banking, insurance, logistics and healthcare alongside retail. GROCERY: not evidenced. Current positioning is vertical-agnostic enterprise AI.
- **Demo/trial:** No public demo/trial; sales-led with a 30:60:90 piloting framework.
- **Vs CAS:** WEAKENING COMPETITOR — the STORY is more useful than the product, and this pass strengthens the original's read. Vue.ai was the canonical fashion-AI-tagging vendor and now sells to HDFC Bank and Tata Motors. Set that against the rest of the segment's consolidation (Syte→Pereg majority stake; ViSenze→Rezolve; Attraqt→Crownpeak; Klevu+Searchspring→Athos; Wide Eyes→Stylitics) and one pattern is unmistakable: FASHION-SPECIFIC AI FEATURES DO NOT SUSTAIN STANDALONE COMPANIES. What IS growing is fashion-specific DATA and MERCHANDISING WORKFLOW (Lily AI, Stylitics) and broad platforms (Algolia, Constructor, Bloomreach). This directly supports LatentView's premise that CAS is a CAPABILITY sold into existing stacks, not a product — and argues its durable value must be anchored in the client's own catalog data and merchandiser workflow, not in the cleverness of the ranker. Their 30:60:90 pilot framing remains a good template for structuring a CAS engagement.

#### Increff — https://www.increff.com/increff-ai

- **What it does:** CORRECTION RETAINED AND RE-VERIFIED: Increff is NOT a search or product-attribution vendor. Re-fetched in this pass and confirmed — it is an AI merchandising planning, demand forecasting and fulfilment platform organized across six modules: Financial Planning (demand forecasting, merchandise financial planning, OTB management), Assortment Planning (range architecture, buying, reordering), Inventory Planning (allocation & replenishment, inter-store transfers, markdown optimization), Warehouse Operations (WMS, cross-docking, cloud warehouse, label software), Order Orchestration (OMS, vendor fulfillment), and Store & Omnichannel (store management, BOPIS/BORIS, endless aisle, self-checkout). [V]
- **Key features:** Demand forecasting at style/store/channel/region granularity [V]; Merchandise financial planning, OTB management, assortment and range architecture [V]; Allocation & replenishment, inter-store transfers, markdown optimization [V]; WMS, cross-docking, OMS, vendor fulfillment systems [V]; Store & omnichannel: BOPIS/BORIS, endless aisle, self-checkout [V]; Mentions "image-based search" but as an INTERNAL retailer analytics feature, explicitly not consumer-facing site search [V]
- **Named clients:** NO named clients and NO performance statistics on the fetched page — re-confirmed in this pass. [V] The page references Gartner and G2 reviews and availability on Google Cloud and Microsoft AppSource marketplaces, but names no customers.; Company-level claim of "700+ global brands" appears only in search results, not on the fetched page [S] — do not quote.
- **Pricing:** NOT PUBLISHED on the fetched page. [V] Listed on Capterra and GetApp, typically indicating a mid-market SaaS motion with quote-based pricing.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** FASHION: strong, but on the SUPPLY side (buying, allocation, inventory), not the demand/discovery side. GROCERY: some coverage via CPG and pharmacy. Sells to planning and supply-chain teams, not digital/e-commerce teams — a different buyer entirely.
- **Demo/trial:** Demo request; profiles on Capterra and GetApp with user reviews — a rare public source of real buyer sentiment in this space.
- **Vs CAS:** NOT A COMPETITOR — the original report's correction was right and I am re-confirming it, because it is the kind of error that costs credibility. Including Increff in a search-vendor list in front of a fashion retailer would signal that the analysis was assembled from category labels rather than actual product understanding. It IS an adjacency and a potential integration story: Increff forecasts demand and allocates inventory, CAS decides what shoppers see, and neither closes the loop today. CAS could surface an inventory-aware or sell-through-aware ranking signal, which answers a real merchandiser objection ("your AI keeps promoting items I'm about to mark down") — and note that Stylitics already ships inventory-aware outfit assembly, so this is a proven requirement, not a speculative one. Also relevant to LatentView's positioning: Increff is an India-HQ'd retail AI platform selling globally into exactly the fashion tier CAS targets, a useful go-to-market credibility reference point.

#### 3DLOOK — https://3dlook.ai/

- **What it does:** Mobile AI body scanning — FitXpress generates 80+ body measurements from phone photos in under 45 seconds, with real-time pose validation and clothing detection. Also offers Mobile Tailor for made-to-measure and uniform fitting.
- **Key features:** Phone-camera 3D body scan, no special hardware [V]; Published accuracy claims: 96-97% accuracy for body measurements, over 95% consistency in repeatability [V]; Weight prediction (3.5% average error), BMI verification (89% accuracy), fat ratio analysis, body progress tracking [V]; Made-to-measure / on-demand manufacturing and uniform fitting applications [V]
- **Named clients:** Logos shown for Bravo, Magic Fit, Vevo, Reddit, CoreDirection — notably NOT major apparel retailers [V]; Claimed retailer outcomes of "4x increase in conversions, 20% higher AOV" appear in third-party coverage rather than as attributed retailer case studies [S] — do not quote.; 3DLOOK's content hub is, however, a widely-cited secondary source for apparel return-rate statistics — useful as a research pointer, not as a client-facing citation.
- **Pricing:** Page references pricing but no rates surfaced. Demo/sales-led.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** FASHION: relevant on paper but WEAKENING — the homepage leads with weight prediction, BMI, fat ratio and body progress tracking, which is health/fitness positioning rather than apparel discovery, and the named logos are not apparel retailers. GROCERY: none.
- **Demo/trial:** Demo/contact only; public content hub at https://3dlook.ai/content-hub/
- **Vs CAS:** LOWEST PRIORITY — include for completeness, not as a competitor or priority partner. The useful signal is negative and worth telling the client lead: 3DLOOK appears to be drifting from apparel fit toward health/body-composition metrics, and camera-scan sizing carries a consent/privacy friction problem that Bold Metrics' no-scan approach sidesteps. If CAS wants a fit partner for a fashion pitch, Fit Analytics (250+ partners, returns data, published averages) or Bold Metrics (no-scan, frictionless, 22 named brands verified) are the credible choices, not 3DLOOK.

**Segment takeaways:**

- *** CORRECTIONS LOG — READ FIRST. THIRTEEN CLAIMS IN THE ORIGINAL REPORT WERE WRONG, STALE OR UNSOURCED. *** (1) KILLED: "Constructor markets on never having lost an A/B test" — Constructor's site makes no such claim; it publishes 98.5% average client retention over three years and a Petco 13% conversion lift (https://constructor.com/). Asserting this to a Constructor customer would be actively damaging. (2) KILLED: "YesPlz is the only vendor with published pricing" — yesplz.ai/pricing publishes NO figures; all three tiers are pay-as-you-go-via-calculator or custom quote. NOT ONE VENDOR IN THIS SEGMENT PUBLISHES A PRICE (Google Cloud aside). (3) KILLED: the "ViSenze $200–500/mo" price band — no primary source, and ViSenze was acquired by Rezolve Ai in Aug 2025, so any pre-acquisition price is stale. (4) CORRECTED: "Syte headcount 11–50, down from ~150" — best available figure is ~100 (2026) vs 158 (2023); this is a contraction, not a collapse. (5) UPGRADED: the Syte acquisition is now PRIMARY-sourced and deck-safe — Pereg Ventures took a majority stake 2024-12-04 with Magma/MizMaa/Stardom, Ziv Ben-Barouch became CEO (PRNewswire). (6) KILLED: Macy's, Gap, Bloomingdale's and thredUP as Lily AI clients — not on any Lily primary page. (7) CORRECTED: Stylitics is "3,600 brands across 100+ retailers," not "4,500 brands and retailers"; and its $80M Series C closed in MARCH 2022, not recently. (8) KILLED: the Stylitics free trial (Capterra claim; official page shows "Book a demo" only). (9) KILLED: Constructor's White Stuff +21% and Bonobos +92% figures — unverifiable on Constructor's site. (10) CORRECTED: "ASOS builds in-house / effectively closed" — ASOS is repeatedly named as an Attraqt/Crownpeak customer. (11) TIGHTENED: the Lily AI retailer is UNNAMED ("a large, multi-brand speciality retailer") and the $22M is explicitly PROJECTED from the measured 3.3% demand lift. (12) CORRECTED: Syte's own pages attribute the same multiples inconsistently (2.5X to Decathlon on the homepage vs 2.3X to Perry Ellis on the fashion page) — further reason to quote none of them. (13) RE-SOURCED: the fit-returns statistic now has real attribution (Coresight 2023: 53%; McKinsey 2024: ~70% of the ~30% of fashion orders returned) — present a RANGE with a named source, never a bare ">50%".
- *** THE BIGGEST OMISSION: DAYDREAM. *** The original report missed the most direct competitor to CAS's exact pitch that exists. Daydream — founded by Julie Bornstein (ex-Stitch Fix COO, ex-Nordstrom, ex-Sephora), $50M seed from Forerunner, Index, GV and True Ventures — launched "Powered by Daydream" on 2026-07-29: natural-language and visual AI search embedded into fashion brands' and retailers' OWN sites, against their own inventory (https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/daydream-launches-ai-powered-search-and-discovery-solution-for-fashion-brands-and-retailers-302837597.html). Live at launch: STAUD, Alice + Olivia, Couper, Cult Mia, Hampden Clothing. Signed: Anine Bing, Mansur Gavriel, ba&sh, Sandro, Maje, MESHKI, Ramy Brook plus 25+ more. WHAT THIS CHANGES: CAS can no longer present LLM natural-language fashion search as novel — a buyer may already have seen this demo. CAS's defensible ground shifts to what Daydream does not evidence: typed hard-constraint extraction with score-ceiling enforcement, per-result reranker reasons, a published eval harness, hybrid BM25 routing into an incumbent keyword stack, and cross-vertical grocery coverage (Daydream is fashion-exclusive and cannot follow CAS there). Daydream's weakness is the enterprise tier — its brands are contemporary/premium, not H&M/C&A scale — which is exactly where LatentView's consulting relationships are the counterweight. ACTION: brief the client lead explicitly, and run CAS's eval queries against daydream.ing before any fashion meeting. Being surprised by this in a room is far worse than raising it first.
- THE CORE ENRICHMENT-VS-RANKER FINDING SURVIVED VERIFICATION INTACT — AND GOT A BETTER PROOF POINT. Every number in Lily AI's flagship case study checked out verbatim: attributes up 33% on average (8→24 on one product), 8.5% lift in discoverability, 3.3% lift in search-driven demand, and the enriched data "deployed into Bloomreach for full implementation" with the ranker unchanged (https://www.lily.ai/resources/customer-stories/ai-product-discoverability/). Two precision fixes: the retailer is UNNAMED and the $22M is PROJECTED from the 3.3% measured lift — lead with the measured numbers, present the $22M as an extrapolation, and you keep the credibility. *** NEW AND BETTER EVIDENCE THE ORIGINAL MISSED: FOOT LOCKER is a named client of BOTH Lily AI (https://lily.ai/industries/fashion) AND Constructor (https://constructor.com/). *** That is a stronger proof of the coexistence architecture than the anonymous Bloomreach anecdote, because both vendors are named and both name the same retailer: enterprise retailers buy the enrichment layer and the ranking layer SEPARATELY and run them TOGETHER. This is the single most slide-ready fact in the entire research pass for CAS's "we sit alongside your incumbent" pitch. The corroborating structural evidence also holds: Syte's 15,000-attribute lexicon, YesPlz's silhouette/mood tagging, and Stylitics ADDING Catalog Enrichment with a published "Stylitics vs. Lily AI" comparison. Nobody in fashion sells a ranker without also selling the data underneath it.
- THE COUNTER-THESIS ALSO SURVIVED — ALL FOUR LOAD-BEARING MARQO QUOTES CONFIRMED VERBATIM, which is unusual in this segment. Marqo argues "the intelligence lives at the product level, not in a layer of rules and signals on top of it," and claims Mejuri saw "a 19.8 percent increase in search-driven conversion after moving to Marqo" plus "over 130 million dollars in attributed revenue uplift for a single retailer, measured through controlled A/B testing" (https://www.marqo.ai/blog/improving-search-relevance-in-fashion). The original also understated their traction: verified client logos include Kicks Crew, Redbubble, Kogan, Shutterstock, SwimOutlet and Poshmark, not just Mejuri. THE DEFENSIBLE SYNTHESIS IS UNCHANGED AND WORTH STATING PLAINLY TO THE CLIENT: enrichment and semantic retrieval solve overlapping problems by different routes, and enrichment wins on BREADTH not depth — a semantic ranker improves ONE surface (site search), while persisted attributes improve site search AND Google Shopping AND Meta Ads AND SEO AND merchandising AND demand forecasting. That breadth, not superior relevance, is why Lily AI can quote 8-9 figure impact off a 3.3% demand lift. CONCRETE RECOMMENDATION (unchanged, still right, now more urgent): add a batch/offline mode to app/translator.py's constraint extractor that writes inferred attributes back as a persisted catalog artifact. CAS already extracts typed constraints and already has an LLM+embedding grounding path in app/recommendations.py — this is a modest lift on existing code that converts CAS from query-time-only into something that can also occupy the enrichment slot. Stylitics entering that category is the signal the window is closing.
- *** A CAS GAP NOBODY HAS NAMED YET, AND THE EVIDENCE IS NOW OVERWHELMING: AGENT-READABILITY / GEO. *** In roughly twelve months this went from a frontier idea to a shipping, sold product line across the entire segment: Athos Commerce sells a GEO Assistant and an Offsite Discovery feed to "AI platforms" (https://athoscommerce.com/pricing/); YesPlz markets "GEO Ready" catalogs "AI agents can read"; Bold Metrics ships an "Agentic Sizing Protocol" branded "The Missing Link in Agentic Commerce"; Lily AI generates schema markup for AI answer engines; Algolia's Gartner Leader positioning is explicitly about "AI-powered, agentic discovery." Meanwhile the rails are real: Stripe and OpenAI co-developed the Agentic Commerce Protocol, and Coach, Kate Spade, Vuori, SKIMS, Glossier, Spanx and the URBN family are live on it (https://stripe.com/newsroom/news/stripe-openai-instant-checkout) — note that Coach, Kate Spade and Vuori are all Lily AI clients, i.e. enrichment vendors are already the on-ramp to agentic channels. NOTE THE VOLATILITY: OpenAI launched in-chat Instant Checkout in Feb 2026 and deprecated it in March 2026; the current ACP model is product discovery plus merchant redirect [S — digitalcommerce360]. So do NOT pitch checkout integration. DO pitch the durable half: making the catalog legible to agents is the same enrichment work CAS should be doing anyway, and CAS's typed-constraint extraction is genuinely well-suited to producing agent-readable structured output. Add GEO/agent-readability to CAS's known-gaps list — it is currently absent.
- *** GROCERY WARNING THAT BELONGS IN THE FASHION REPORT BECAUSE IT UNDERMINES CAS'S SIGNATURE FEATURE: GOOGLE IS SHIPPING RECIPE-TO-CART. *** The original report treated Google purely as a demand-side force (Lens). It missed that Google Cloud sells Vertex AI Search for commerce — managed retail search, browse and recommendations tuned for retail including fashion, supporting up to 10,000 custom attributes (https://cloud.google.com/solutions/vertex-ai-search-commerce) — and that Google's conversational commerce materials demo a shopper saying "I need the ingredients for lasagna" with the AI populating the cart including brand follow-ups. ALBERTSONS — a named P0 CAS target — built its "Ask AI" tool with Google on exactly this. CAS's recipe/ingredient-card shopping list is its most differentiated capability and it is NOT unique against a hyperscaler a grocery buyer may already run. CAS's answer must be DEPTH, not novelty: per-ingredient brand alternatives in a tab carousel, typed dietary constraint enforcement with score-ceiling capping ("meat dinner but no chicken"), and a published completeness eval against a 70% ingredient-coverage target — none of which Google publishes. Rehearse the answer to "why not just use Vertex?" because it will be a first-meeting question. Also note Vertex is the ONLY vendor in this landscape with genuinely public pricing — pull it as a cost-modelling reference for CAS's commercials.
- VISUAL SEARCH REMAINS CAS'S BIGGEST FASHION GAP, THE GOOGLE LENS NUMBER IS BETTER SOURCED NOW, AND THERE IS A NEW H&M-SPECIFIC WRINKLE. Verified framing: Google Lens handles more than 25 billion queries per month with roughly 1 in 5 carrying commercial intent — stated by Dan Taylor, VP of Global Advertising at Google, at a press briefing on 2026-03-12 (https://www.asiae.co.kr/en/article/2026031214442621001). It is still a VP quote via secondary outlets rather than a Google-primary publication, so attribute it that way; and per the original's own advice, do not use Pinterest Lens figures at all, they vary by an order of magnitude. ASOS shipped Style Match in 2017-18 across 85,000+ products; Syte, YesPlz and Daydream are built around visual search; ViSenze→Rezolve shows it becoming a platform feature rather than a category. *** NEW AND IMPORTANT IF CONFIRMED: third-party coverage claims H&M's in-app visual search already accounts for over 12% of mobile product searches, with camera users converting 15% faster and ~8% higher baskets [S — secondary industry blogs only, NO H&M primary source found]. VERIFY BEFORE USING *** — if true, visual search is a baseline expectation at H&M rather than a differentiator to pitch. HANDLE HONESTLY: do not claim CAS covers visual search. DO make the architectural argument — CAS's retrieval is already embedding-based and modality-agnostic, so swapping gte-small for a CLIP-class multimodal encoder is a contained change in app/embeddings.py, since search.py, the reranker and the constraint layer all operate on vectors regardless of origin, and products.csv already carries img_url. Build the image-search spike BEFORE any fashion demo. It remains the highest-leverage gap-closer in this vertical.
- CAS'S BEST UNCLAIMED FASHION ADVANTAGE IS STILL COLD START, AND MARQO'S VERBATIM FRAMING IS THE BEST AVAILABLE ARTICULATION OF IT: "New products are discoverable from day one. Because the model understands each product based on its content, not its transaction history, a dress added to your catalog this morning ranks correctly for every relevant style query immediately." ASOS adds 5,000 items a week; fast fashion turns over continuously. Behavioural/click-driven incumbents — Bloomreach's "self-driving search" builds profiles from every search, purchase and click — need weeks of interaction data before a new SKU ranks properly. CAS's content-based embedding + LLM rerank path ranks a brand-new item correctly on day one. This is concrete, testable with CAS's EXISTING eval harness (a genuinely cheap experiment: hold out newly-added SKUs and measure), and directly answers the fast-fashion buyer's real pain. It should be a headline in an H&M/C&A pitch, not a footnote. It is also the one CAS advantage that no vendor in this landscape can neutralise with a feature release, because it is a property of the architecture rather than a module.
- TARGET-ACCOUNT INTELLIGENCE — THE COMPETITION IS ALREADY INSIDE BOTH P0 VERTICALS, AND THE GROCERY SIDE IS NEWLY MAPPED. FASHION: C&A is CONFIRMED as a named client on Syte's fashion page (https://www.syte.ai/ecommerce-fashion/), so a CAS pitch there is displacement-or-coexistence, not greenfield; C&A's CEO Paulo Corrêa has AI as a board-level priority with the CTO reporting directly to him, already runs logged-in personalization by purchase history, size and style preferences, and is publicly discussing AI agents negotiating with consumers' digital assistants (https://neofeed.com.br/negocios/na-ca-a-ia-sai-do-bastidor-e-vai-para-a-vitrine/en/). H&M's public posture is about product-data foundations — Global Product Information Lead Adam Ull describes a "flexible MACH Architecture built on our Product Data Hub" with microservices and a single source of truth (https://www.ecommerceexpo.co.uk/news/hms-global-product-information-lead-enhancing-customer-experience-ai-data) — so the H&M entry point is the ENRICHMENT/data-layer story, not the ranker story. *** CORRECTION: ASOS IS NOT CLOSED. *** The original claimed ASOS builds in-house and is effectively unreachable; ASOS is repeatedly named as an Attraqt/Crownpeak customer, meaning large European fashion retailers DO buy third-party discovery — an argument FOR CAS's addressable market. Zalando genuinely is largely closed (own models plus OpenAI LLMs across 25 markets) but note the newly-found adoption data the original said did not exist: ~6 million customers used Zalando's assistant across all of 2025, and nearly 10 million in Q1 2026 alone, a roughly fourfold YoY increase [S — RetailDetail/xpert.digital, not Zalando-primary]. *** NEW: GROCERY ACCOUNTS ARE ALSO TAKEN. *** Kroger is a named client of Empathy.co (https://empathy.co/) and Albertsons built its Ask AI with Google Cloud. Both P0 grocery targets already have an incumbent AI discovery partner.
- *** A RISK TO CAS'S OWN ARCHITECTURE THAT NO ONE HAS RAISED: EU DATA SOVEREIGNTY. *** Empathy.co — omitted entirely from the original report — has built an entire enterprise market position on "Enterprise AI Search made for Commerce" running "under EU governance and free from any third party or big tech provider," and won Kroger, Ocado and Carrefour with it (https://empathy.co/). The existence of a viable vendor whose primary differentiator is "no big tech, EU-governed" is direct evidence that European retail buyers raise this objection and that it is contract-relevant. CAS currently runs on Groq-hosted gpt-oss-120b — a US inference provider. For C&A (Brazil/Germany), H&M (Sweden) or Carrefour, a data-residency requirement could disqualify the current deployment outright, regardless of relevance quality. THE GOOD NEWS: app/llm_client.py already reads provider config DYNAMICALLY through the cfg.X pattern, so swapping to an EU-hosted or self-hosted model is a configuration change, not a re-architecture — and the embedding model (gte-small, ~33M params, CPU) can run entirely on the client's own infrastructure with no external call at all. That is a strong answer IF REHEARSED and a weak one if improvised. Prepare it before any European client meeting. SECONDARY BENEFIT: Empathy.co publishes open grocery and books NER models on Hugging Face, including a Groceries GLiNER and a Grocery NER Dataset — a direct, inspectable analogue to CAS's dietary-constraint extraction. Benchmarking CAS against them is cheap and is the only true apples-to-apples competitive comparison available anywhere in this landscape.
- THE TWO DISQUALIFYING ENTERPRISE GAPS ARE CONFIRMED — BUT ONE OF THE ORIGINAL'S SUPPORTING FACTS WAS FALSE, SO MAKE THE ARGUMENT DIFFERENTLY. (1) NO MERCHANDISING CONSOLE — fully verified and this is the strongest gap evidence in the report: Syte ships an AI Tag Editor ("Review AI-generated tags and add or remove tags according to your merchandising and brand guidelines"), a Lexicon Manager to "Tailor your taxonomy by mapping hundreds of product categories, attributes and values," Thematic Tags, Tag Analytics, and a unified console to "Set ranking strategies, merchandising and business rules, and run A/B tests" (https://www.syte.ai/ai-tagging-merchandising/). In fashion, merchandisers OWN ranking — brand guidelines, seasonal pushes and markdown priorities are non-negotiable business inputs. A system that cannot be overridden by a merchandiser will not be bought, regardless of relevance scores. (2) NO A/B FRAMEWORK — still disqualifying, but *** DROP the "Constructor never lost an A/B test" line, it is false ***. Make it with verified facts instead: Lily AI's entire Lily Max loop runs on "matched A/B tests with holdout groups" and difference-in-differences measurement; Syte's console has A/B built in; Marqo's headline $130M claim is explicitly "measured through controlled A/B testing." Online experimentation is the currency of proof in this market. CAS's offline eval suite (P@10/MRR/NDCG@10, stated R@10 caveats, an explicit label-leakage probe) is more methodologically transparent than anything any vendor here discloses and is a genuine consulting differentiator — but it is NOT the same artifact as online experimentation and must never be presented as if it were.
- A THIRD GAP STILL MISSING FROM CAS'S OWN LIST: SEASONALITY AND TREND FRESHNESS. Stylitics runs a "living taxonomy" with an in-house team monitoring "social media, influencer channels, and niche forums" plus "Nightly feed updates and AI-powered tagging [to] keep your catalog current with zero manual lift" (https://stylitics.com/resources/blog/trend-tagging/). Zalando runs Trend Spotter across ten fashion capitals weekly (https://corporate.zalando.com/en/technology/zalando-brings-its-ai-powered-assistant-all-markets-and-adds-four-new-cities-its-trend). CAS has no trend signal, no recency weighting, and an embedding cache keyed to a static CSV content hash that re-encodes only on full catalog swap — architecturally the opposite of nightly refresh. In a vertical defined by micro-trend cycles this is a real objection. Note it compounds with the cold-start ADVANTAGE in an interesting way worth pitching: CAS ranks new items correctly on day one from content, which is half the freshness problem solved for free; what it lacks is the incremental re-indexing pipeline to get those items INTO the index nightly. That reframes the gap from "CAS can't do fresh" to "CAS needs an incremental indexing path," which is an engineering task with a known shape, not an architectural flaw. Add it to the known-gaps list before a client finds it first.
- MARKET STRUCTURE — THE CONSOLIDATION EVIDENCE IS NOW MUCH STRONGER THAN THE ORIGINAL REPORT SHOWED, AND IT VALIDATES LATENTVIEW'S PREMISE. Six transactions, all verifiable: Attraqt → Crownpeak (~£63.2M, closed Dec 2022); Wide Eyes → Stylitics; Syte → Pereg Ventures majority stake (2024-12-04, PRNewswire, new CEO); Klevu + Searchspring → Athos Commerce (closed 2025-01-09, PSG Equity); ViSenze → Rezolve Ai (2025-08-27); and Vue.ai's pivot from canonical fashion-tagging vendor to horizontal enterprise AI selling to HDFC Bank, Tata Motors and Microsoft (https://www.vue.ai/). NOTE ALSO that PSG Equity backs BOTH Athos Commerce and Stylitics — the same growth-equity firm assembling positions across discovery and visual merchandising. THE LESSON: fashion-specific AI FEATURES do not sustain standalone companies, but fashion-specific DATA (Lily AI), MERCHANDISING WORKFLOW (Stylitics) and BROAD PLATFORMS (Algolia — Gartner MQ Leader three years running; Constructor and Bloomreach — Forrester Wave Q3 2025 Leaders) do. This strongly supports the premise that CAS is a CAPABILITY sold into existing stacks rather than a product, and argues its durable value must be anchored in the client's own catalog data and merchandiser workflow, not in the cleverness of the ranker. A SECOND-ORDER IMPLICATION FOR THE PITCH: consolidation means fewer standalone slots to compete for but MORE integration work per deal — which is consulting revenue, and is precisely LatentView's business rather than a software vendor's.
- GO-TO-MARKET SHAPE IS VALIDATED — SELL THE ROUTER, NOT THE RANKER — AND THE PROOF IS NOW NAMED RATHER THAN ANONYMOUS. Bloomreach and Constructor are Forrester Wave Q3 2025 Leaders (9 vendors, 31 criteria; Netcore Unbxd a Strong Performer with the highest Current Offering score; Rezolve Ai a Contender), and Algolia is a Gartner MQ Leader for the third consecutive year — these are what H&M/C&A/Kroger-tier retailers actually run. Lily AI did not replace Bloomreach, it enriched the data feeding it; and Foot Locker independently appears as a named client of both Lily AI and Constructor. CAS's hybrid keyword-first router (BM25 first, AI pipeline only on lexical miss) is the single best asset for this conversation because it is the one component that says "we are not asking you to rip anything out" — it should be the CENTERPIECE of a fashion pitch, not a footnote. It also neutralises CAS's ~7s warm latency, which is otherwise unshippable against sub-second incumbents on a primary PLP path: as a fallback-only route, 7s applies to queries that currently return NOTHING, where the alternative is a null-results page. Say that explicitly and the latency weakness becomes a scoped tradeoff rather than a disqualifier. PULL BOTH ANALYST REPRINTS FREE via the vendor gates (https://visit.bloomreach.com/forrester-wave-search-2025, https://info.constructor.com/forrester-wave-2025-commerce-search, https://www.algolia.com/lp/gartner-magic-quadrant-2026) — they are the best free maps of this market's evaluation criteria and tell you exactly which capabilities buyers are scored against.
- PRICING INTELLIGENCE — THE HONEST FINDING IS THAT THERE IS NO PUBLIC PRICE IN THIS MARKET, AND THE ORIGINAL REPORT'S TWO ANCHORS WERE BOTH WRONG. I directly checked the two vendors the original cited as exceptions: YesPlz's pricing page publishes NO figures (three tiers, all pay-as-you-go-via-calculator or custom quote), and the ViSenze $200–500/mo band has no primary source and is stale post-acquisition. Athos Commerce — whose Klevu heritage once had a transparent ~$449/mo entry point per third-party sources — now publishes no figures and no free trial either; the mid-market tier has moved behind a quote wall (https://athoscommerce.com/pricing/). Syte, Lily AI, Stylitics, Vue.ai, Fit Analytics, Bold Metrics, 3DLOOK, Daydream, Crownpeak/Attraqt, Constructor, Bloomreach and Algolia are all quote-only. TWO GENUINELY USEFUL DATAPOINTS SURVIVE: (a) Stylitics discloses its MODEL — "Our pricing is customized to your catalog size, channels, and business goals" with a "Usage-Based Model: Pricing scales with your catalog and activations" (https://stylitics.com/pricing/) — catalog-size-plus-activations is a sensible template for structuring CAS commercially; and (b) Nosto reportedly prices on GMV rather than query volume [S], a second viable model worth considering since it aligns vendor and retailer incentives. (c) Google Cloud's Vertex AI Search for commerce publishes usage-based rates in GCP documentation — the only real public price in the landscape and the best available cost-modelling reference. RECOMMENDED IMMEDIATE ACTIONS: (1) run CAS side-by-side against Daydream (daydream.ing) and YesPlz (https://poc.yesplz.ai/demo) on the same queries; (2) pull the Forrester and Gartner reprints; (3) benchmark CAS's dietary-constraint extraction against Empathy.co's open Grocery GLiNER models on Hugging Face; (4) grab Bold Metrics' free Fit & Sizing Buyer's Guide to see how fashion buyers actually score this category.
- *** EVIDENCE-QUALITY WARNING — THE ORIGINAL REPORT'S OWN WARNING WAS RIGHT AND THIS PASS PROVED IT EMPIRICALLY. *** I attempted to verify the marquee performance multiples and found Syte's OWN PAGES attributing the same-shaped numbers to DIFFERENT CLIENTS (homepage: 2.5X CVR + 40% AOV to Decathlon, 7.1X to Coleman, 829% ARPU to Chow Sang Sang; fashion page: 2.3X against Perry Ellis, 470% ARPU, 8X for Venca). When a vendor's own site cannot keep attribution consistent, none of those numbers belong in a client deck. THE TIERING TO USE: MOST CREDIBLE — Lily AI's case (controlled test, tagged vs untagged, ranker held constant, methodology described, retailer unnamed, revenue explicitly projected); Fit Analytics' modest 4-6% conversion / 2-4% returns averages (small numbers are a credibility signal, not a weakness); Constructor's Petco 13% and 98.5% retention; Marqo's $130M explicitly "measured through controlled A/B testing." LEAST CREDIBLE — any "Nx conversion" multiple with no stated methodology; these are almost certainly engaged-user-vs-all-user comparisons rather than randomised lift, and a sophisticated buyer will say so out loud. ITEMS THAT REMAIN UNVERIFIED AND MUST NOT BE QUOTED: Empathy.co↔Inditex (CB Insights only — high value if true, verify first); H&M's 12% visual-search share (secondary blogs only); Stylitics' Macy's/Kohl's/Revolve/Puma/Express client names and the $4B/3,600-brands figures (PSG and Retail TouchPoints both returned HTTP 403); all Latka/Tracxn ARR and headcount figures; Lily AI's 28% Google Shopping and 21.4% Meta ROAS figures (WWD redirect-blocked); Klevu's $449/mo; ViSenze's client names; Increff's "700+ brands"; Bold Metrics' Men's Wearhouse 47.4%; and all Pinterest Lens volumes. FINALLY, THE POSITIVE VERSION OF THIS POINT — CAS's own published eval methodology (P@10/MRR/NDCG@10 with explicit caveats about why R@10 is intentionally tiny, a stated label-leakage probe, a stress/injection suite, and reproducible provider and embedding comparisons) is MORE RIGOROUS AND MORE TRANSPARENT THAN ANYTHING ANY VENDOR IN THIS SEGMENT DISCLOSES. In a consulting sale where the buyer has been burned by unfalsifiable "7.1X" claims, methodological transparency is not a technical detail — it is the differentiator. Show the harness, show the caveats, show a number that is only 3% and explain why you trust it. That is a posture no vendor in this landscape can copy.

**Sources:** https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/daydream-launches-ai-powered-search-and-discovery-solution-for-fashion-brands-and-retailers-302837597.html — [FETCHED] BIGGEST GAP FILLED: "Powered by Daydream" launched 2026-07-29; live brands STAUD, Alice + Olivia, Couper, Cult Mia, Hampden Clothing; signed Anine Bing, Mansur Gavriel, ba&sh, Sandro, Maje, MESHKI, Ramy Brook + 25 more; 1.5M shoppers out of beta; 325 retailers / 10,000+ brands / 3M products; pricing not disclosed · https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/daydream-secures-50-million-in-seed-funding-to-launch-new-ai-powered-search-and-discovery-shopping-platform-302177481.html — [SEARCH RESULT] Daydream $50M seed, 2024-06-20, Forerunner/Index/GV/True Ventures; founder Julie Bornstein (ex-Stitch Fix COO) · https://www.lily.ai/resources/customer-stories/ai-product-discoverability/ — [FETCHED, RE-VERIFIED] Confirmed verbatim: +33% attributes (8→24 on one product), 8.5% discoverability lift, 3.3% demand lift, $22M PROJECTED, deployed "into Bloomreach for full implementation", retailer UNNAMED ("a large, multi-brand speciality retailer") · https://lily.ai/industries/fashion — [FETCHED] Verified client logos incl. Coach, M&S, J.Crew, Kate Spade, UGG, HOKA, Fabletics, Vuori, Bombas, Tecovas, Simkhai, Arhaus, FOOT LOCKER, HIBBETT SPORTS, NARS, Shiseido, Clé de Peau, Drunk Elephant. Macy's/Gap/Bloomingdale's/thredUP NOT present — killed from the report · https://constructor.com/ — [FETCHED] CORRECTION SOURCE: no "never lost an A/B test" claim exists; actual claims are 98.5% avg client retention over 3 years, 4.8 G2, 4.9 Gartner Peer Insights, and Petco 13% conversion lift. Named clients incl. Sephora, Under Armour, Gap, REI, FOOT LOCKER, Bonobos, Rothy's · https://www.syte.ai/ecommerce-fashion/ — [FETCHED, RE-VERIFIED] C&A CONFIRMED as a named client (direct hit on P0 target). Also Prada, PrettyLittleThing, Decathlon, Farfetch, Perry Ellis, Falabella, Baycrews, Venca, Tally Weijl, Nalli Silks · https://www.syte.ai/ — [FETCHED] Stat-attribution inconsistency found: homepage gives Decathlon 2.5X CVR + 40% AOV, Coleman 7.1X, Chow Sang Sang 829% ARPU — conflicts with the fashion page's attributions. Basis for the "quote none of these" warning · https://www.syte.ai/ai-tagging-merchandising/ — [PREVIOUSLY FETCHED, RETAINED] AI Tag Editor, Lexicon Manager, Thematic Tags, Tag Analytics, unified console for "ranking strategies, merchandising and business rules, and run A/B tests" — the concrete shape of CAS's merchandising-console gap · https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/pereg-ventures-takes-majority-stake-in-syte-to-empower-apparel-ecommerce-with-ai-powered-product-recommendations-302322957.html — [FETCHED] PRIMARY SOURCE, UPGRADES A PREVIOUSLY-UNVERIFIED CLAIM: Pereg Ventures majority stake 2024-12-04 with Magma VC/MizMaa/Stardom; Ziv Ben-Barouch (Pereg co-founder) becomes CEO; "more than 100 customers worldwide"; no revenue/headcount disclosed · https://athoscommerce.com/pricing/ — [FETCHED] GAP FILLED + PRICING CORRECTION: Klevu+Searchspring merged entity; three quote-based plans (Onsite/Offsite/Complete Discovery), AI Agents incl. a GEO Assistant, NO published figures, NO free trial · https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20250113743474/en/Klevu-Joins-Forces-with-Searchspring-to-form-Athos-Commerce-Creating-a-Leading-Comprehensive-Global-AI-Backed-Ecommerce-Optimization-Platform — [SEARCH RESULT] Searchspring (PSG Equity) acquired Klevu, closed 2025-01-09, forming Athos Commerce; full platform launch Q3 2025 · https://empathy.co/ — [FETCHED] GAP FILLED: KROGER (P0 grocery target), Ocado, Casa del Libro, Motive Market named; Carrefour/Vodafone/BSH logos; "EU governance and free from any third party or big tech provider"; open Groceries GLiNER + Grocery NER Dataset on Hugging Face; fashion NOT listed as a vertical · https://www.cbinsights.com/company/empathyco/customers — [SEARCH RESULT, AGGREGATOR — UNVERIFIED] Lists Inditex alongside Kroger and Carrefour as Empathy.co customers. HIGH VALUE IF TRUE but absent from Empathy.co's own site — DO NOT QUOTE WITHOUT CONFIRMATION · https://www.crownpeak.com/resources/blogs/crownpeak-enters-agreement-to-acquire-attraqt/ — [SEARCH RESULT] GAP FILLED + CORRECTION SOURCE: Attraqt (incl. Fredhopper/XO) acquired by Crownpeak, ~£63.2M, finalised Dec 2022; 300+ brands; clients reported as ASOS, JD Sports, Screwfix, Calvin Klein, Tommy Hilfiger — contradicts the original's "ASOS builds in-house / is closed"

### Cloud/platform search products + the build-vs-buy picture (Google, Azure, AWS, Elastic, Vespa, Databricks, Shopify, commercetools, SAP, Salesforce, Adobe) — plus market size, contract values, Gartner 2026 MQ for Search & Product Discovery, and published ROI evidence. FACT-CHECKED AND EXPANDED: ~35 load-bearing claims independently re-verified; 8 corrected, 4 killed as unsourceable, 1 vendor added.

#### Google Cloud — AI Commerce Search (formerly Vertex AI Search for commerce / Agent Search for commerce) — https://docs.cloud.google.com/retail/docs/what-is-it

- **What it does:** Fully managed retail-specific search/browse/recommendations API. Retailer uploads a product catalog plus a user-event stream (search, click, add-to-cart, purchase); Google handles preprocessing, model training/tuning, serving and scaling. NAMING VERIFIED AND CORRECTED: the docs now say verbatim 'Formerly known as Agent Search for commerce, the service has been rebranded for ecommerce websites and mobile applications.' The current product name is AI Commerce Search; 'AI Commerce Search in Gemini Enterprise for Customer Experience' is the CONSOLE option used to configure it, not the product's primary name — the original report overstated this as a full rebrand. Capabilities confirmed in docs: Search (combines search and recommendation for personalized experiences), Browse (guided search with dynamic filtering that adapts to the initial query), Recommendations (ML models), and conversational filtering that guides users to refine broad queries. Separately, the GA Conversational Commerce agent shipped 10-11 Sept 2025 — multi-turn NL discovery, guided prompts, personalized recommendations.
- **Key features:** Semantic + keyword hybrid retrieval with query understanding, spell correction, intent recognition; Search, Browse and Recommendations as separate billable surfaces; Conversational Commerce agent (GA Sept 2025): multi-turn conversations that adapt to customer needs, guided prompts to clarify/refine, 'vs canned answers from a chatbot'; Conversational filtering — AI-guided narrowing of broad queries; Merchandising console for non-technical business users (a CAS gap); Built-in A/B testing and experimentation (a CAS gap); Semantic image search / multimodal discovery (a CAS gap); Continuous learning from user events; personalization from behaviour + location
- **Named clients:** Albertsons Companies — VERIFIED, first retailer to market with the Conversational Commerce agent, shipped as 'Ask AI' in the search bar of all banner apps: Albertsons, Safeway, Vons, Jewel-Osco, Shaw's, Acme, Tom Thumb; Galeries Lafayette — VERIFIED via Grid Dynamics case study (335 stores, 600K+ SKUs incl. Mirakl marketplace, 60M+ annual visitors); Adorama, Michaels, Nordstrom — cited in the original report via Grid Dynamics; I did NOT independently verify these three, treat as unconfirmed; Cotopaxi — KILLED. The original report claimed 'search conversion +44.78% after replacing Fast Simon with Google Vertex.' I could not reach any page substantiating this. Do not put it in a client deck.
- **Pricing:** Consumption-based. $2.50 per 1,000 search & browse requests; predictions/recommendations from $0.27 per 1,000 (first 20M/month tier); training & tuning $2.50 per node-hour; $600 free trial credits. SOURCING STATUS AFTER RE-CHECK: I attempted BOTH https://cloud.google.com/products/retail/pricing and https://cloud.google.com/retail/pricing directly and both truncated before the pricing table rendered — the original researcher hit the same wall, so this is a genuine tooling limit, not sloppiness. The $2.50/1K, $0.27/1K, $2.50/node-hour and $600-credit figures are corroborated by the indexed search snippet of cloud.google.com/retail/pricing itself plus https://digitalsuits.co/blog/vertex-ai-search-new-gen-ecommerce-search-for-multiple-use-cases/. Directionally reliable; get a Google rep to confirm in writing before it goes on a slide. Billing gotcha for buyers: every re-rank or new result set is a billable call (https://nimstrata.com/ai-commerce-search-pricing-calculator assumes 3-5 billable events per session). INFERENCE (arithmetic checked): 20M searches/month x $2.50/1,000 = $50,000/month = $600,000/year on search alone, before recommendations.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** Strongest fit of any vendor here for BOTH target verticals — which is the problem. GROCERY: Albertsons is the flagship logo, exactly LatentView's target tier. Google reports customers using Ask AI 'often add one or more additional items to their cart, uncovering products they might not have found otherwise' — precisely the basket-building behaviour CAS's recipe feature targets. NOTE: the original report's '>85% of Ask AI conversations start with open-ended/exploratory questions' stat did NOT appear in any page I fetched — treat as unverified and drop it. FASHION: Galeries Lafayette results VERIFIED verbatim on the Grid Dynamics page — 7% total revenue increase, 8% higher average basket value, 20% YoY online sales growth in peak periods, plus '2X faster execution' for the merchandising team.
- **Demo/trial:** Yes — $600 free trial credits; Conversational Commerce agent accessible in the console; product page at https://cloud.google.com/solutions/vertex-ai-search-commerce. Production onboarding requires Google Cloud sales.
- **Vs CAS:** OVERLAP (near-total): query understanding, semantic retrieval, ranking, recommendations, multi-turn conversational discovery. WHAT GOOGLE HAS THAT CAS DOES NOT: merchandising console, built-in A/B testing, semantic image search, session personalization from event streams, and a live grocery reference at Albertsons. WHAT CAS HAS: (a) per-result human-readable reason strings — Google returns ranked products, not generated justifications; (b) typed hard-constraint extraction with score-ceiling capping, auditable and overridable per constraint; (c) the recipe/shopping-list surface with per-ingredient brand alternatives; (d) relevance-gated sponsored ads structurally separate from organic with the bid never exposed; (e) no per-query meter and no event-stream dependency on Google. GARTNER STATUS — NEW, AND IMPORTANT: Google was named a LEADER in the Gartner MQ for Search and Product Discovery dated 24 June 2025 (https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/ai-machine-learning/gartner-magic-quadrant-for-search-and-product-discovery). I could NOT verify Google's placement in the 12-vendor 2026 MQ (22 June 2026) — do not claim 'Google is a 2026 Leader' without checking the actual document. POSITIONING: the Galeries Lafayette case is the template for the consultancy play — Grid Dynamics did NOT replace Vertex, they wrapped it with a custom Merchandising Experience Platform plus automated catalog/pricing/stock ingestion pipelines because the merch team faced growing manual workload and limited experimentation. That is the honest LatentView pitch.

#### Microsoft Azure AI Search (underpins Foundry IQ) — https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/agentic-retrieval-overview

- **What it does:** General-purpose managed retrieval service, not retail-specific: BM25 + vector + hybrid (RRF) indexes, a Bing-derived semantic reranker, and an 'agentic retrieval' multi-query pipeline. It is a retrieval PLATFORM, not a product-discovery application — no catalog schema, no merchandising UI, no event-based personalization, no recommendations.
- **Key features:** Agentic retrieval (VERIFIED verbatim): an LLM 'break[s] down a complex query into smaller, focused subqueries', subqueries 'run simultaneously and can be keyword, vector, or hybrid search', 'each subquery undergoes semantic reranking', then results are combined into a unified response with optional source references and an activity log; Reasoning effort configurable minimal / low / medium; at 'minimal' the LLM query-planning step is SKIPPED and queries go straight to knowledge sources (defaults to 'low'); Semantic ranker: L2 rerank of top BM25/RRF results using multilingual Bing-derived models, returns @search.rerankerScore 0-4 plus verbatim captions/answers; synonym maps applied during reranking; Knowledge bases + knowledge sources abstraction; MCP endpoint for agent-to-agent use; Handles 'questions with multiple asks', conversation-dependent context, LLM-generated paraphrasing, and spelling mistakes (docs' own example is a multi-constraint hotel query); Storage-optimized L1/L2 tiers for large vector workloads
- **Pricing:** FULLY VERIFIED — I re-pulled the live meters from the Azure Retail Prices API (East US) and every figure in the original report checks out exactly. Basic $0.101/SU-hour; Standard S1 $0.336; S2 $1.344; S3 $2.688; Storage-Optimized L1 $3.839; L2 $7.677 (confidential-compute 'CC' variants run ~10% higher). Semantic Ranker $1.00 per 1,000 queries. Agentic Retrieval reasoning tokens $0.000022 per 1K (minimal and low effort) and $0.0001 per 1K (medium); free-tier meters exist at $0.00. TWO METERS THE ORIGINAL REPORT MISSED: 'Semantic Ranker Unit' at $16.12/DAY (a fixed provisioned charge) and 'Semantic Ranker Overage Queries' at $2.00/1K — the $1.00/1K headline is not the whole cost. Azure OpenAI is billed SEPARATELY for query planning and answer synthesis. Microsoft's own worked example (verified in-doc): 2,000 agentic retrievals, 3 subqueries each, 50 chunks reranked per subquery, 500 tokens/chunk = $3.30 search-side + $0.60 input + $0.42 output = $4.32 total. Use the Retail Prices API, not the marketing page, which renders dollar figures as '$-' placeholders.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** Weak as a packaged fit; strong as infrastructure for Microsoft-standardised retailers. No retail catalog model, no merchandising console, no discovery accelerator. Microsoft's docs position the semantic ranker for prose-shaped, information-rich content — short Amazon-style product titles are its weakest input, which is exactly the failure mode CAS already documented for HyDE. Realistically it appears in fashion/grocery deals as the retrieval layer an SI builds a discovery app on.
- **Demo/trial:** Yes — Free tier (50MB, 3 indexes), Azure portal wizards, and agentic-retrieval quickstarts in C#/Java/JavaScript/Python/TypeScript/REST plus an end-to-end tutorial and Azure-Samples repos.
- **Vs CAS:** STILL THE MOST DIRECT ARCHITECTURAL THREAT TO CAS'S CORE CLAIM, and the verification strengthened rather than weakened this. Azure's agentic retrieval is functionally CAS's pipeline: LLM decomposes query into subqueries -> parallel retrieval per subquery -> semantic rerank per subquery -> merge. CORRECTION TO THE GA CLAIM: the docs say 'SOME agentic retrieval features are generally available in the 2026-04-01 REST API via programmatic access' — it is NOT wholesale GA, portal and Foundry-portal access remain preview-only, and a further tranche sits in the 2026-05-01-preview API. Say 'partially GA via REST since 2026-04-01' and you are accurate. WHAT CAS STILL HAS: (a) Azure's semantic ranker reranks a bounded top-N and cannot rerun the query over the corpus — CAS's deep RERANK_POOL_K over a scatter-gather union is better recall-shaped; (b) Azure returns a 0-4 score plus a verbatim EXTRACTIVE caption, not a GENERATED one-sentence reason; (c) no hard-constraint typing/score-capping, no cross-sell/upsell grounding, no sponsored-ad layer, no recipe decomposition; (d) Azure is content-shaped (RAG, copilots), not commerce-shaped — no catalog, no ratings blend, no merchandising. POSITIONING: do not pitch 'we do multi-intent retrieval' as the differentiator. LATENCY COVER (verified quote): 'Agentic retrieval adds latency compared to a single-query pipeline, but it handles query complexity that a single query can't.' Microsoft conceding this is your permission slip for a multi-second AI path behind a fast lexical router.

#### AWS — Amazon OpenSearch Service (managed + Serverless + S3 Vectors) — https://aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/pricing/

- **What it does:** Managed Elasticsearch/OpenSearch: BM25 lexical search, k-NN/vector indexes, hybrid search, faceting, typo tolerance. Pure infrastructure — no retail catalog model, no relevance-as-a-service, no merchandising UI, no LLM layer. Retailers use it as the substrate and build or buy everything above it.
- **Key features:** Managed domains (dedicated instances) or Serverless (OCU-based auto-scaling); k-NN vector indexes; hybrid lexical+vector; GPU-accelerated vector index build; S3 Vectors — cheap cold vector storage tier queried via API; NextGen collections (VERIFIED on the pricing page): 'There is no minimum OCU requirement — Indexing and Search OCUs scale to zero after 10 minutes of inactivity'; Classic collections still bill 'at least for a minimum of 2 OCUs' for the first collection; UltraWarm / OR1 managed storage tiers for cost control
- **Pricing:** VERIFIED on the AWS pricing page: c6g.large.search $0.113/hr, r6g.xlarge.search $0.335/hr, or1.xlarge.search $0.418/hr. Storage: UltraWarm and OR1 managed storage $0.024/GB-month; EBS gp2/gp3 $0.122-$0.135/GB-month. S3 Vectors: $0.06/GB-month storage, $0.20/GB PUT, $2.50 per million queries, plus tiered query processing $0.004-$0.002/TB. CORRECTION: the original report's '$0.24 per OCU-hour' for Serverless did NOT appear on the page I fetched — the page describes the NextGen no-minimum/scale-to-zero and classic 2-OCU-minimum billing model but does not surface the OCU rate in the retrievable content. Quote the model, not the $0.24, unless you re-confirm it. The '28 May 2026 GA' date for NextGen Serverless was also not on the pricing page — drop the date, keep the capability. INFERENCE (arithmetic checked): a 3-node r6g.xlarge cluster = 3 x $0.335 x 730 = ~$734/month of compute before storage — an order of magnitude below metered SaaS search at scale.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** Neutral/enabling. Most large US grocers and fashion retailers already run OpenSearch or Elasticsearch somewhere — it is the incumbent keyword stack CAS is designed to sit alongside. AWS ships no fashion- or grocery-specific relevance model, no apparel attribute extraction, no recipe/basket logic.
- **Demo/trial:** Yes — AWS Free Tier (750 hours/month t2/t3.small.search for 12 months) and console-based domain creation; vector-search workshops documented at https://aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/
- **Vs CAS:** OVERLAP: minimal at the intelligence layer — this is the layer BELOW CAS. CAS's own BM25 engine and keyword-first router mirror what a retailer already has in OpenSearch, which is the integration story: route to the existing lexical stack, escalate to the AI pipeline only on a lexical miss. WHAT AWS DOES THAT CAS DOES NOT: horizontal scale, HA, sub-50ms p99, operational tooling, security/compliance. POSITIONING: for AWS-standardised retailers pitch CAS as the intelligence layer over their existing cluster — no rip-and-replace, no new per-query meter, infra cost already sunk. RISK: AWS is a credible 'good enough' answer for a CTO who says 'we already have OpenSearch hybrid search' — the rebuttal must be reranker reasons, hard constraints and merchandisable outputs, not 'semantic search'.

#### AWS — Amazon Personalize — https://aws.amazon.com/personalize/pricing

- **What it does:** Managed recommendation/personalization service: user-personalization, similar-items, personalized ranking (re-ranks a candidate list for a given user), and user segmentation. The complement to OpenSearch — AWS's answer to personalized product discovery without a search engine. Personalized-Ranking-v2 is the closest AWS analogue to a reranker, but it ranks on BEHAVIOURAL signals, not query semantics. VERIFIED: no deprecation or end-of-support notice on the pricing page as of Aug 2026.
- **Key features:** Personalized-Ranking-v2 — reranks a supplied candidate set per user; User-Personalization-v2 — personalized item recommendations; Similar-items / related-items recommendations; Use-case-optimised 'Recommenders' with simpler hourly pricing; Real-time event ingestion + batch inference; Trained on the retailer's own interaction data (cold start is a known weakness)
- **Pricing:** VERIFIED in full. Data ingestion $0.05/GB. v2 recipes: training $0.002 per 1,000 interactions ingested; real-time AND batch inference $0.15 per 1,000 requests. Custom solutions: training $0.24/hour; real-time tiered at $0.0556/1K (first 72M/mo), $0.0278/1K (next 648M), $0.0139/1K (over 720M); batch tiered $0.067/1K (first 20M), $0.058/1K (next 180M), $0.050/1K (over 200M). Use-case-optimised recommenders per 100K users: $0.375/hr first 100K, $0.045/hr next 900K, $0.018/hr next 9M, $0.005/hr over 10M. User segmentation $0.016 down to $0.001 per 1,000 users. CORRECTION TO THE FREE TIER: it is not open-ended — the page states the free tier applies 'for the first two months' and covers up to 20GB/month data processing, 5M interactions/month training for v2 recipes (or 100 training hours for others), and 50,000 real-time requests/month for v2 recipes (180,000 for others). The original report implied a standing 5M-interactions allowance.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** Decent for grocery (high repeat purchase, dense interaction data = Personalize's best case), weaker for fashion (seasonal catalog churn, cold start on every drop). No named fashion/grocery logos published on the pricing page.
- **Demo/trial:** Yes — two-month free tier as above, plus console-based getting-started flows at https://aws.amazon.com/personalize/
- **Vs CAS:** OVERLAP: cross-sell/upsell and the ranking-blend step. Personalized-Ranking-v2 does what CAS's Bayesian-rating blend and cross-sell module do, but from real behavioural data rather than an LLM proposing complements. WHAT PERSONALIZE DOES THAT CAS DOES NOT: genuine session/user personalization — an admitted CAS gap, solved here for $0.15 per 1,000 requests. WHAT CAS DOES THAT PERSONALIZE DOES NOT: it has NO query understanding at all. It cannot interpret 'breathable outfit for a humid day' or 'meat dinner but no chicken' — it needs a candidate set handed to it. USE IN THE PITCH: this is the honest answer to 'what about personalization?' — CAS for intent, Personalize (or the client's CDP) for identity, LatentView as the integrator. Do not pretend CAS competes on personalization.

#### Elastic (Elasticsearch + ELSER) — https://www.elastic.co/industries/retail-ecommerce

- **What it does:** The incumbent search stack at a very large share of retailers, with a semantic layer bolted on: ELSER (Elastic's sparse-vector retrieval model, no fine-tuning), the semantic_text field type that auto-generates embeddings at index time, a production vector DB, hybrid/RRF ranking, and (2026) Agent Builder and Workflows for agentic patterns. Explicitly a DIY toolkit — Elastic's own e-commerce guide is an implementation tutorial, not a packaged retail product.
- **Key features:** ELSER — out-of-the-box semantic retrieval, no domain fine-tuning required; semantic_text field type: embeddings generated automatically at index time; Hybrid BM25 + vector with RRF; reranking via inference endpoints; Elastic Inference Service (token-priced) and Elastic Managed LLM; Agent Builder + Workflows (2026) for agentic retrieval patterns; Runs on-prem, any cloud, or Elastic Cloud Serverless — data-residency friendly
- **Named clients:** Kroger — VERIFIED, named directly on elastic.co/industries/retail-ecommerce as a customer spotlight; H-E-B — VERIFIED AND UPGRADED. The original report hedged this as 'seen in a search snippet.' I fetched the page: H-E-B is named on it with a linked case study. You can quote it.; Walmart — VERIFIED on the same page (the original report missed this; note it is a benchmark, not a target buyer); Home Depot; Shopify; The Warehouse
- **Pricing:** VERIFIED on https://www.elastic.co/pricing/serverless-search — every figure in the original report is correct. Search VCU from $0.09/hr, Ingest VCU from $0.14/hr, Machine Learning VCU from $0.07/hr; storage & retention from $0.047/GB-month; egress from $0.05/GB. Elastic Managed LLM $4.50 per million input tokens / $21 per million output tokens; Elastic Inference Service from $0.08 per million tokens. Agent Builder 1,000 executions free then $0.025/execution; Workflows 10,000 free then $0.0108/execution. Support as a percentage of consumption: Standard included, Gold 5%, Platinum 10%, Enterprise 15%. NEW AND USEFUL FOR THE BUILD-VS-BUY SLIDE — Elastic's own worked examples: a dev environment with 2GB searchable data is $24-27/month and a PRODUCTION environment with 20GB searchable data is $190-210/month depending on Search Power. That is a devastating cost anchor against metered SaaS search. Self-managed Elasticsearch remains free (Elastic License / AGPL).
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** Very strong incumbency in grocery specifically — Kroger AND H-E-B are both named on Elastic's own retail page, and those are two of the three named target buyers. Elastic markets a '77% improvement in website conversion rates' across retail deployments; I checked the page and CONFIRM the claim appears there and CONFIRM that no methodology, sample or source is disclosed for it — it sits under a gated 'Elastic Results That Matter' download. Unaudited vendor claim; do not repeat it as a fact. Elastic's e-commerce semantic-search blog uses grocery examples ('whole fish for grilling', 'pop' meaning soda) almost identical in spirit to CAS's demo queries.
- **Demo/trial:** Yes — 14-day Elastic Cloud free trial, free self-managed download, and a fully worked e-commerce semantic search tutorial with notebooks at https://www.elastic.co/search-labs/blog/semantic-search-elasticsearch-ecommerce
- **Vs CAS:** OVERLAP: semantic retrieval, hybrid BM25+vector, reranking — all shipped, and ELSER means a retailer gets 'semantic search' with roughly a schema change and no model training. That materially raises the bar for what counts as differentiated. WHAT ELASTIC DOES NOT DO: no LLM query decomposition into typed intents with hard constraints; no per-result generated reason; no commerce semantics (ratings shrinkage, cross-sell grounding, sponsored separation, recipe decomposition); no merchandising console. HIGHEST-VALUE INSIGHT, NOW FULLY SOURCED: Kroger and H-E-B are named Elastic customers on Elastic's own site. The realistic CAS deal at those accounts is not 'replace your search', it is 'add the intent/reranking/explanation layer on top of the Elasticsearch you already run' — exactly what CAS's keyword-first router was built for. Lead with that, with the link.

#### Vespa.ai — https://vespa.ai/industries/e-commerce/

- **What it does:** Open-source (Apache 2.0) big-data serving engine unifying structured filtering, lexical search, vector/tensor search and ML model inference inside the ranking pipeline, with multi-phase ranking so a cheap first phase runs over millions and an expensive model over the survivors. Most often chosen by retailers who decide to build rather than buy. Available as Vespa Cloud managed service or self-hosted.
- **Key features:** VERIFIED verbatim: 'Hybrid keyword and semantic search combine precise lexical matching with natural language understanding'; VERIFIED verbatim: 'Apply merchandising rules, promotional campaigns, inventory awareness, and ranking models together'; VERIFIED verbatim: 'Deploy production-ready AI models directly within the retrieval and ranking pipeline'; VERIFIED verbatim: 'AI-powered shopping assistants that understand customer intent, answer product questions, compare alternatives'; Multi-phase ranking — cheap first phase, ML second phase, controlling infra cost; Real-time behavioural signals + customer context + inventory as ranking inputs; Self-hosted or Vespa Cloud with autoscaling
- **Named clients:** Vinted — VERIFIED, named with a linked case study; Kleinanzeigen — VERIFIED; Groupon — VERIFIED; Decathlon — VERIFIED; Otto — VERIFIED; IMPORTANT CAVEAT: the Vespa e-commerce page carries NO quantified results for any of these logos — no conversion lift, no benchmark numbers. Use the logos, not implied outcomes.
- **Pricing:** VERIFIED on https://cloud.vespa.ai/price-calculator.html — every figure in the original report is exact. Startup $0.05/vCPU-hr, $0.005/GB-mem-hr, $0.0002/GB-disk-hr, $0.03/GB-GPU-mem-hr. Basic $0.10 / $0.01 / $0.0004 / $0.07. Commercial $0.145 / $0.0145 / $0.0005 / $0.10. Enterprise $0.18 / $0.018 / $0.0007 / $0.125, with a stated 'Minimum monthly spend of $20,000'. Volume discounts reduce unit prices up to 50% (83% on enclave); a year-long spend commitment gives 15% off (8% on enclave) with no prepayment. Self-hosted is free (Apache 2.0). (The 'default quota $10/hour' detail was not retrievable on the page I fetched — the page defers quota specifics to a separate reference; drop it.)
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** Good fashion/marketplace evidence — Vinted, Otto, Decathlon and Kleinanzeigen are all apparel/general-merch marketplaces at very large scale, and Decathlon and Otto are the closest analogues to a C&A/H&M-tier buyer. No named grocery logo found.
- **Demo/trial:** Yes — Vespa Cloud free trial with $300 credits and no credit card (https://vespa.ai/free-trial/), plus an open-source end-to-end e-commerce sample app using an Amazon product dataset at https://github.com/vespa-engine/sample-apps
- **Vs CAS:** OVERLAP: retrieval + ranking mechanics, done better than CAS's numpy/cosine implementation — multi-phase ranking, tensor ops, real-time updates, horizontal scale that a 300K-row in-memory approach cannot match. WHAT VESPA DOES NOT DO: no LLM layer, no query understanding, no intent expansion, no generated reasons, no merchandising UI, no recipe logic. It is an engine, not an application — though note it now markets 'AI-powered shopping assistants that understand customer intent', so the engine vendors are climbing the stack too. STRATEGIC READ: Vespa is the most credible engine choice FOR CAS ITSELF at enterprise scale — moving CAS's retrieval onto Vespa (or Databricks Vector Search) while keeping the LLM translator/reranker/commerce layer answers the 'does this scale past 300K SKUs?' objection without conceding the differentiated layer. The verified $20,000/month Enterprise minimum is also a useful floor price for 'serious retail search' that makes a consulting engagement look cheap.

#### Databricks — Mosaic AI Vector Search (+ Agent Bricks) — https://www.databricks.com/product/pricing/vector-search

- **What it does:** Vector database and hybrid retrieval built into the Databricks lakehouse, kept in sync with Delta tables under Unity Catalog governance. Directly relevant because CAS is migrating to Databricks. Supplemented by Agent Bricks for building/evaluating/optimising governed agents.
- **Key features:** Delta Sync indexes (Databricks-managed or self-managed embeddings) that stay in sync with the source table; HNSW ANN vector index; Full-Text Search index (BETA) using Okapi BM25; Hybrid search via Reciprocal Rank Fusion with rrf_param fixed at 60; Filtering, reranking, and metadata columns supported; Unity Catalog governance and lineage on the retrieval layer; Agent Bricks; Genie for NL analytics; Lakebase for unified POS/e-comm/supply-chain data
- **Named clients:** Grupo Casas Bahia — VERIFIED named on the retail solutions page; 7-Eleven — VERIFIED; Burberry — VERIFIED; Coop — VERIFIED; Fonterra — VERIFIED; CRITICAL CAVEAT, RE-CONFIRMED: Databricks publishes NO quantified results for any of these customers on that page. Logos only.
- **Pricing:** VERIFIED on the pricing page. Standard endpoint 4.00 DBU/hour supporting ~2M vectors at 768 dimensions; Storage-Optimized 18.29 DBU/hour supporting ~64M vectors at 768d. Effective US East rate $0.07/DBU (AP Sydney $0.088-0.09/DBU). Databricks' own worked example, verified: 5 million vectors = 3 units x 720 hours x 4.00 DBU/hr x $0.07/DBU = $605/month in US East. Billed on endpoint hours regardless of QPS, plus embedding generation billed per token at ingestion. This is ON TOP of the client's existing Databricks commit.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** Burberry (luxury fashion) plus Coop and 7-Eleven (grocery/convenience) are credible logos in both verticals. Databricks' retail page markets 'Deploy conversational AI agents that guide product discovery, answer questions and support transactions with real-time inventory and context' — i.e. they are marketing the CAS value proposition to the CAS buyer. CORRECTION: the original report also attributed 'intent-based discovery' to the retail section. It is not there — that phrase appears in the TRAVEL/HOSPITALITY section of the same page ('chat-based booking, intent-based discovery and AI concierge support'). Quote the conversational-agents line, not the intent-based-discovery line, or you will be caught.
- **Demo/trial:** Yes — 14-day Databricks free trial; Vector Search quickstarts and notebooks at https://docs.databricks.com/aws/en/generative-ai/vector-search
- **Vs CAS:** MOST IMPORTANT ENTRY FOR THE ENGINEERING ROADMAP. ALL THE HARD CONSTRAINTS RE-VERIFIED IN THE DOCS AND CONFIRMED: hybrid keyword-similarity search and full-text search are BOTH capped at 200 RESULTS per query, versus 10,000 for pure ANN vector search — CAS's scatter-gather across N intents plus a deep RERANK_POOL_K must fit inside that ceiling. The BM25 full-text INDEX is BETA and available exclusively on storage-optimized endpoints (though query_type='FULL_TEXT' can be used on existing indexes on both endpoint types). Storage-optimized endpoints add 'a slightly increased latency of about 250msec'. Delta Sync row limit 100KB; embedding source column capped at 32,764 bytes; max embedding dimension 4,096 (gte-small at 384 is fine). Standard endpoints comfortably hold 300K products. The fixed rrf_param=60 means CAS's keyword-first router is re-implementable natively — worth benchmarking against the self-contained BM25 engine. STRATEGIC RISK: Agent Bricks + Genie + Lakebase means Databricks is assembling the same stack CAS occupies inside the platform the client already pays for. The counter: Databricks ships primitives and governance, not a merchandisable retail search application with reasons, hard constraints, sponsored separation and recipe decomposition. LatentView builds that.

#### Shopify — Search & Discovery (native semantic search) — https://help.shopify.com/en/manual/online-store/storefront-search/search-and-discovery-modify-search

- **What it does:** Shopify's first-party, FREE storefront search app, now including semantic search that uses related words, concepts, categories and product IMAGE data to expand results beyond keyword matching, plus merchandising controls and complementary/related product recommendations.
- **Key features:** Semantic search using product attributes including 'the product's description, and image data, such as text that's part of the image itself and colors' (VERIFIED verbatim); Activates AUTOMATICALLY when eligibility criteria are met — no configuration required in the app; Synonym groups; product boosts against specific search terms; Multi-criteria filters (price, brand, colour, size); Related and complementary product recommendations; Search analytics dashboard and conversion tracking; Autocomplete / instant search, typo tolerance, custom ranking
- **Pricing:** FREE (first-party Shopify app) — VERIFIED on the App Store listing. Gating is by plan and catalog size, not price, and ALL THREE LIMITS ARE VERIFIED VERBATIM in Shopify's help docs: semantic search requires a Grow, Advanced or Plus plan; 'Your store has fewer than 200,000 products'; 'Semantic search capability isn't supported for the Japanese locale'; and 'Semantic search capability doesn't apply to predictive search.' App Store rating VERIFIED at 2.8/5 from 457 reviews, with a notably polarized distribution — 52% five-star (236 reviews) against 19% one-star (87 reviews). A July 2026 review also flags a 25-filter limit as pushing sophisticated storefronts to third-party solutions.
- **Fashion/grocery fit:** Very high relevance to small/mid fashion brands on Shopify; largely irrelevant to Kroger/Albertsons/H-E-B-tier grocers who are not on Shopify. The sub-200,000-product ceiling is the key fact — it excludes essentially every enterprise grocery catalog and most department stores, which is why the enterprise search vendors still exist.
- **Demo/trial:** Yes — free install from the Shopify App Store on any dev store: https://apps.shopify.com/search-and-discovery
- **Vs CAS:** OVERLAP: semantic search, synonyms, boosts, complementary recommendations — all free. PRICING PSYCHOLOGY: 'AI semantic search' now has a $0 anchor for anyone on Shopify. WHAT SHOPIFY CANNOT DO (and CAS can): catalogs over 200K products (CAS is demoed on 300K); typed hard constraints; per-result reasons; recipe/ingredient decomposition; relevance-gated sponsored ads; multi-intent expansion; Japanese locale; typeahead semantics. WHAT SHOPIFY DOES THAT CAS DOES NOT: image-data-informed retrieval (CAS has no visual search), a merchandising UI, and a search analytics dashboard. USE DEFENSIVELY: when a CTO says 'Shopify does this for free', the answer is the 200K ceiling, the plan gating, the predictive-search exclusion, and the 2.8/5 rating — all four now verifiable with links.

#### commercetools — Storefront Search (Product Catalog) + AI Hub — https://commercetools.com/blog/b2b-product-spotlight-storefront-search

- **What it does:** Search built natively into the commercetools composable commerce platform in three modes: Lexical (BM25), Semantic (AI embeddings over product names, descriptions, categories and configurable attributes), and Hybrid (both, fused with RRF). Positioned as a TCO play — no separate search systems or data pipelines.
